Upon this rich history and commitment, we are building a family and a life together. In the same manner, our faith towards God should progress. We grow from knowing about God, to knowing God, committing to Him (accepting His salvation), then building our life upon that relationship.

### Faith Produces Obedience and Good Works

Our trusting relationship with God is strengthened through obedience, the result brining good works! These works don’t save us or earn God’s love-they’re the natural result of a life rooted in Him.

Scripture presents a natural progression of faith. As we come to know God and experience His presence, we grow in our belief in Him. This belief compels us to act in ways that align with our faith, leading us to obey His commands. Our obedience produces good works, which bear a harvest of positive and beneficial outcomes.

Consider again this passage from Romans:


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="10" data-verses="16-17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Romans 10:16-17</u>** (NKJV) **16*** But they have not all obeyed the gospel. For Isaiah says, "LORD, WHO HAS BELIEVED OUR REPORT?" **17** So then faith comes by hearing, and hearing by the word of God.*

</span>


Dissecting this in reverse order:

1.  Hearing the Word (**rhema**) of God produces faith (**pistis**)

2.  Faith, which is a state of belief or persuasion, will produce the action or verb of believing.

    1.  Again, consider the person who believes they can be debt free acts upon it!

3.  Our act of believing is demonstrated in obedience.

The word for ‘obeyed’ is **hupakouō (***hoop-ak-oo'-o **- G5219)***: **hupakouō** means to *hear under* (as a *subordinate*), that is, to *listen attentively*; by implication to *heed* or *conform* to a command or authority: - hearken, be obedient to, obey.

In a work setting when the boss or supervisor is giving instructions, they are not “talking to talk”, rather they are giving directions that they intend their employees to follow.

When we really listen to God, it should lead us to action-to trust Him and obey. As the scriptures say in:


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="James" data-chapter="1" data-verses="22" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>James 1:22</u>** (NKJV) *But be doers of the word, and not hearers only, deceiving yourselves.*

</span>


Consider again the example of financial independence. A person in debt that longs to be free of the burden will seek out financial counseling. The counselor will teach them about the principles of financial freedom and help them build a budget. This counseling will (trust / faith) in what they need to do be debt free. But confidence is just the first step, the person must obey the principles they’ve learned through disciplined practice to receive the good work and blessing of being debt free.

The book of James says that our belief (faith) must produce works! We already know what works is (e.g., ‘dead’ works from <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="6" data-verses="1" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 6:1</span>).


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="James" data-chapter="2" data-verses="14-20" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>James 2:14-20</u>** (NKJV)
>
>**14** *What does it profit, my brethren, if someone says he has faith but does not have works? Can faith save him?*
>
>***15** If a brother or sister is naked and destitute of daily food,*
>
>***16** and one of you says to them, "Depart in peace, be warmed and filled," but you do not give them the things which are needed for the body, what does it profit?*
>
>***17** Thus also faith by itself, if it does not have works, is dead.*
>
>***18** But someone will say, "You have faith, and I have works." Show me your faith without your works, and I will show you my faith by my works.*
>
>***19** You believe that there is one God. You do well. Even the demons believe-and tremble!*
>
>***20** But do you want to know, O foolish man, that faith without works is dead?*

</span>


What stands out to you from that passage in James? To me a significant sentence is that even the demons believe! Belief alone is not enough. Our belief must rise to the level of obedience. We need to and want to obey Jesus our risen savior and Lord.

We do not receive salvation through works! When we receive salvation (through faith) we do works! Let me reiterate that, you cannot work hard enough, or make enough sacrifices to earn your salvation, it is the free gift of God that we receive by faith!

The outcome of these works (obedience) is that they yield a good and beneficial harvest. The length of time it takes to receive the harvest, or the measure of that harvest is varied, but it will produce a good harvest! (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Genesis" data-chapter="8" data-verses="22" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Genesis 8:22</span>).

Recalling back to our study on sin and sacrifices, we know that several of the sacrifices instituted in the Old Testament were for sin, so that man could reconcile himself to God or his fellow man. However, there is a more desirable path!

<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="1 Samuel" data-chapter="15" data-verses="22" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> ** <u>1 Samuel 15:22</u>** (NKJV) *So Samuel said: "Has the LORD as great delight in burnt offerings and sacrifices, As in obeying the voice of the LORD? Behold, to obey is better than sacrifice, And to heed than the fat of rams.*

</span>

A truth consistent since the Old Testament is that obedience is better than sacrifice. It's better to correctly follow what we should do, than to need to make a sacrifice to make up for what we did not do correctly. Thankfully there is grace from God when we stray from the path, miss the mark (sin), and need to turn back towards God.

As we build our faith in God, it will naturally produce in us obedience. This obedience is to our benefit and produces a good harvest. Going back to my person example from this chapter, I would not have experienced the peace in the season, or the blessing of a promotion if I had not obeyed God's word to me to *wait on Him*!

### Benefits of Obedience

It's very important in the life of every believer that they strengthen their faith (trust) towards God. God has wonderful promises and blessings for everyone who obeys Him, too numerous to mention here, but these are a few of them:

1.  **Blessings and Provision**

<span class="x-scripture" data-type="fragment" data-book="Deuteronomy" data-chapter="28" data-verses="1-14" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Deuteronomy 28:1-14</u>** (NKJV) - "If you fully obey the Lord your God and carefully follow all his commands… all these blessings will come on you and accompany you…"*

</span>


2.  **Guidance and Wisdom** 

<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Psalm" data-chapter="32" data-verses="8" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Psalm 32:8</u>** (NKJV) - "I will instruct you and teach you in the way you should go; I will counsel you with my loving eye on you."*

</span>


3.  **Answered Prayers**

<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="1 John" data-chapter="3" data-verses="22" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>1 John 3:22</u>** (NKJV) - "And whatever we ask we receive from him, because we keep his commandments and do what pleases him."*

</span>


4.  **Protection and Safety**

<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Psalm" data-chapter="34" data-verses="7" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Psalm 34:7</u>** (NKJV) - "The angel of the Lord encamps around those who fear him, and he delivers them."*

</span>


5.  **Joy and Peace**

<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Psalm" data-chapter="119" data-verses="165" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Psalm 119:165</u>** (NKJV) - "Great peace have those who love your law, and nothing can make them stumble."*

</span>


6.  **Fruitfulness and Prosperity**

<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Isaiah" data-chapter="1" data-verses="19" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Isaiah 1:19</u>** (NKJV) - "If you are willing and obedient, you will eat the good things of the land."*

</span>


7.  **God’s Favor**

<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Psalm" data-chapter="37" data-verses="23-24" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Psalm 37:23-24</u>** (NKJV) - "The Lord makes firm the steps of the one who delights in him; though he may stumble, he will not fall, for the Lord upholds him with his hand."*

</span>


8.  **Strength and Endurance**

<span class="x-scripture" data-type="fragment" data-book="Isaiah" data-chapter="40" data-verses="31" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Isaiah 40:31</u>** (NKJV) - "But they that wait upon the Lord shall renew their strength; they shall mount up with wings as eagles…"*

</span>


9.  **Blessing on Future Generations** 

<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Exodus" data-chapter="20" data-verses="6" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Exodus 20:6</u>** (NKJV) - "But showing love to a thousand generations of those who love me and keep my commandments."*

</span>


10. **Eternal Life** 

<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="2" data-verses="7" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Romans 2:7</u>** (NKJV) - "To those who by persistence in doing good seek glory, honor and immortality, he will give eternal life."*

</span>


Every one of those blessings is by the grace of God; we cannot earn them, but we can walk in them through our obedience to Him, which is spurred on and activated by our trusting relationship in Him.

### Practical Steps for Growing Your Faith

Walking with God is relational, but there are skills to be learned if you want good relationships. In earthly relationships, the skills that yield the strong and secure relationships are built upon:

- Open and free dialogue and communication

- Healthy conflict resolution

- Commitment

- Consistency

You must show up consistently, reveal (communicate) yourself (our thoughts and emotions), receive the other person (listen), and walk with them, if you want a strong, vibrant relationship! All these skills apply to our relationship with God.

Beyond core relational skills, there are specific practices and disciplines that will strengthen your relationship with God:

- **Build and Maintain a Prayer Life, Listen and Communicate with God.** Regular, heartfelt prayer is essential to a close relationship with God. Through prayer, you can share your worries, joys, and gratitude, seeking His guidance and strength. (*<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Philippians" data-chapter="4" data-verses="6-7" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Philippians 4:6-7</span>*) Being sensitive to the Holy Spirit’s voice helps you discern God’s will, find wisdom in decisions, and grow in spiritual maturity. Take time for quiet reflection to hear His direction. (*<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="8" data-verses="14" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 8:14</span>*)

- **Spend Time in Worship.** Worship is a powerful way to draw close to God, expressing love and reverence. Through praise, song, or quiet reflection, worship deepens your awareness of His presence. (*<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Psalm" data-chapter="100" data-verses="2-4" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Psalm 100:2-4</span>*)

- **Read, Study, and Meditate on Scripture.** God’s Word reveals His character, promises, and instructions for life. Dedicating time to studying Scripture helps you grow in faith, understanding, and spiritual resilience. (*<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="2 Timothy" data-chapter="3" data-verses="16-17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">2 Timothy 3:16-17</span>*)

- **Obey His Commandments.** Obedience to God’s instructions demonstrates your love, respect, and trust in Him. Walking in obedience aligns your life with His purpose and brings blessings. (*<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="14" data-verses="15" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 14:15</span>*)

- **Actively Attend a Local Church and Serve.** Engaging in a church community offers spiritual support, encouragement, and opportunities to serve others. Being involved helps build a sense of belonging and strengthens your commitment to God’s mission. (*<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="10" data-verses="24-25" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 10:24-25</span>*)

- **Build Relationships with Other Believers.** Fellowship with other Christians provides accountability, encouragement, and shared wisdom. These relationships enrich your faith journey and help you grow through mutual support. (*<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Proverbs" data-chapter="27" data-verses="17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Proverbs 27:17</span>*)

Our ‘faith towards God’ is not a one-time occurrence for salvation but is an ongoing practice and relationship that we are supposed to cultivate our entire lives. The benefits of a trusting and beneficial relationship are incredibly numerous, but we must do the good work of fostering that trust and growth to see it thrive.

## Personal Reflection Questions 

1.  What does faith mean to you personally? How has your understanding of faith grown through this chapter?

2.  Reflect on the concept of logos and rhema. Has there been a time in your life when a specific Scripture came alive for you and became a personal rhema word? How did it impact your faith?

3.  How does understanding faith as persuasion rather than a mystical force shape your relationship with God?

4.  Obedience is described as the fruit of faith. Can you think of a time when your obedience to God deepened your trust in Him? What was the result?

5.  What practical steps can you take this week to cultivate your faith through prayer, Scripture study, and fellowship?

## Group Discussion Questions *(20-30 Minutes)*

1.  **Icebreaker Question:** “When you think of someone with great faith, who comes to mind and why?”

2.  **Exploring Scripture:** Read <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="10" data-verses="17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 10:17</span>. What does this verse teach us about how faith grows? How can we apply it in our daily lives?

3.  **Faith in Action:** What does it mean for faith to produce obedience? Share examples of how obedience has strengthened your faith or someone else’s faith journey.

4.  **Logos and Rhema Discussion:** Discuss the difference between logos (the written word) and rhema (the revealed word). How can we position ourselves to hear and receive rhema words from God?

5.  **Cultivating Faith:** Discuss the practical disciplines of prayer, Bible study, and fellowship. Which discipline has been most effective in growing your faith, and why?

# Chapter 7 - Jesus-The Foundation of Our Faith

Building on our study of Jesus as the Messiah, in this chapter we continue in looking at the life, person, and work of Jesus. We’ll see that Jesus is the Mediator of a new and better covenant-and that He perfectly fills the roles of Prophet, Priest, and King. It is in this New Covenant that we as believers have good standing with God and transition from a works-based covenant to a trust-based (faith-based) covenant. Finally, we will read and study the *Apostles Creed* as a way to round-out our study on Jesus.

## Mediator of the New Covenant

The concept of a mediator involves a go-between who bridges the gap between two parties, facilitating a relationship or agreement. In the Exodus story, Moses is the initial mediator of the covenant God established with His people. Moses received the law, offered the sacrifice (priest), called God’s people to obedience (prophet), and judged them (judge / king). In the remainder of the Old Testament after Moses, the mediating role was fulfilled through the role of the Prophet, the Levitical Priesthood, and the Judges and later Kings of the nation.

Old Testament Roles of Mediators:

1.  **Prophet:** Prophets were God’s messengers, sent to speak His heart to the people and call them back when they wandered. Moses, for example, served as a prophet who mediated the Old Covenant by receiving the Law from God and teaching it to Israel. Prophets also foretold the coming of the Messiah and the ultimate redemption of humanity. Other prominent examples of prophet include: Isaiah, Jeremiah, Elijah, Elisha, Samuel.

2.  **Levitical Priesthood:** Priests acted as mediators by offering sacrifices for the atonement of sins and interceding for the people before God. The High Priest, especially, played a vital role on the Day of Atonement, entering the Holy of Holies to sprinkle blood on the mercy seat, symbolizing reconciliation with God. The priest's ministry is to intercede for the people.

3.  **Judge/King:** Judges, and later Kings, were charged with ruling over God’s people, enforcing His laws, judging between the people, delivering them from their enemies, and leading them in righteousness. The book of Judges tells the stories of these leaders-people like Deborah, Samson, and Gideon-whom God raised up to rescue and guide Israel. Israel later had a line of kings, many wicked, some good (see 1st and 2nd Kings, 1st and 2nd Samuel, and 1<sup>st</sup> and 2<sup>nd</sup> Chronicles). Some prominent examples are Saul, David, and Solomon.

Worth noting is that all the Old Covenant mediators were fully man and not divine. As men they were able to sin, and many times were given to sin. Some of the mediators were sinful and drove people away from God and towards profane worship: false prophets, lying and corrupt priests, and wicked kings.

### Christ as the Mediator of the New Covenant

Jesus’ death, resurrection and ascension fulfil the Old Covenant, and establish the New Covenant.

The New Covenant is a transformative relationship between God and His people, established through the life, death, and resurrection of Jesus Christ. Unlike the Old Covenant, which was focused on external laws, the New Covenant emphasizes internal transformation and grace. It was inaugurated by Jesus at the Last Supper, where He declared that His blood would establish this covenant, replacing the repeated animal sacrifices of the Old Covenant with His once-for-all atonement (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Luke" data-chapter="22" data-verses="20" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Luke 22:20</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="9" data-verses="12" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 9:12</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="10" data-verses="10" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 10:10</span>). Through this sacrifice, the New Covenant provides complete forgiveness and cleansing from sin, fulfilling the requirement for a perfect and permanent reconciliation with God (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Jeremiah" data-chapter="31" data-verses="34" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Jeremiah 31:34</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="8" data-verses="12" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 8:12</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="10" data-verses="12-18" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 10:12-18</span>).

One of the central features of the New Covenant is its internal nature, as God writes His law on the hearts of His people, fostering a heart-level relationship with Him through the Holy Spirit (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Jeremiah" data-chapter="31" data-verses="33" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Jeremiah 31:33</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="8" data-verses="10" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 8:10</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="2 Corinthians" data-chapter="3" data-verses="3" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">2 Corinthians 3:3</span>). This shift enables believers to live out God’s will not by mere adherence to external commands but through empowerment by the Spirit (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="8" data-verses="1-4" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 8:1-4</span>). The New Covenant also grants direct access to God, eliminating the need for an earthly priesthood. Jesus serves as the eternal High Priest, and believers are called a royal priesthood, able to confidently approach God through Christ’s sacrifice (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="7" data-verses="24-25" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 7:24-25</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="10" data-verses="19-20" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 10:19-20</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Peter" data-chapter="2" data-verses="9" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Peter 2:9</span>).

Unlike the Old Covenant, which was exclusive to Israel, the New Covenant is universal, extending to all who believe in Christ, regardless of ethnicity or background (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="3" data-verses="16" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 3:16</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Galatians" data-chapter="3" data-verses="28" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Galatians 3:28</span>). This covenant is based entirely on grace, offering salvation and forgiveness freely through faith in Jesus (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="2" data-verses="8-9" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 2:8-9</span>). It is not sustained by human effort but by the enabling power of the Holy Spirit, who transforms and empowers believers to live in obedience and reflect God’s character (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ezekiel" data-chapter="36" data-verses="26-27" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ezekiel 36:26-27</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="8" data-verses="9-16" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 8:9-16</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Galatians" data-chapter="5" data-verses="22-23" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Galatians 5:22-23</span>). Furthermore, the New Covenant is everlasting, surpassing the temporary and conditional nature of the Old Covenant (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Jeremiah" data-chapter="32" data-verses="40" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Jeremiah 32:40</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="8" data-verses="13" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 8:13</span>). It represents the ultimate fulfillment of God’s redemptive plan, reconciling humanity to Himself and enabling believers to live as His people under His rule for eternity.

Jesus is explicitly identified as the Mediator of the New Covenant in 

<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="9" data-verses="15" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Hebrews 9:15</u>** (NKJV) - *For this reason Christ is the mediator of a new covenant, that those who are called may receive the promised eternal inheritance*.

</span>


Through His atoning death and resurrection, Christ inaugurates this new covenant, replacing the old system of sacrifices and external laws with a covenant written on the hearts of believers (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Jeremiah" data-chapter="31" data-verses="31-34" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Jeremiah 31:31-34</span>). He stands between God and humanity, not only interceding on behalf of His people but also providing the means of reconciliation through His own blood (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="9" data-verses="12" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 9:12</span>). Unlike the mediators of the Old Covenant, Jesus is sinless, eternal, and fully capable of fulfilling the divine requirements for justice and mercy, ensuring a lasting relationship between God and believers.

Jesus Fulfills and Perfects These Roles

1.  **Prophet:** Jesus is the ultimate Prophet, not merely a messenger of God’s word but the Word made flesh (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="1" data-verses="14" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 1:14</span>). He reveals God’s character, will, and plan for salvation fully and perfectly (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="1" data-verses="1-2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 1:1-2</span>). His teachings, miracles, and prophetic declarations fulfill the Old Testament prophecies and provide the foundation for the New Covenant.

2.  **Priest:** Jesus is the perfect High Priest, offering Himself as the once-for-all sacrifice for sin (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="7" data-verses="27" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 7:27</span>). Unlike the priests of the Old Covenant who had to repeatedly offer sacrifices for their own sins and those of the people, Jesus, being sinless, offered His own blood to secure eternal redemption (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="9" data-verses="14" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 9:14</span>).

3.  **King & Judge:** Jesus is the eternal King, reigning in righteousness and justice. As the Son of David, He fulfills the promise of an everlasting kingdom (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="2 Samuel" data-chapter="7" data-verses="16" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">2 Samuel 7:16</span>). His kingship is not merely earthly but cosmic, ruling over all creation and establishing peace through His victory over sin and death (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Philippians" data-chapter="2" data-verses="9-11" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Philippians 2:9-11</span>). Jesus will return as king and judge, and judge ‘both the living and the dead’ (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="10" data-verses="42" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 10:42</span>).

In Christ, the roles of Prophet, Priest, and King converge and are fulfilled perfectly. As the Mediator of the New Covenant, He bridges the gap between a holy God and sinful humanity, enabling believers to approach God with confidence (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="4" data-verses="16" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 4:16</span>) and ensuring the eternal inheritance promised in Him.

## The Apostles Creed

The *Apostles Creed* is not found in scripture, it was created and modified based on the earliest teachings of the church. A creed is a formal confession of beliefs, think of it like a pledge or oath combined with a statement of belief. Creeds are important because they are used to unify and separate those who believe in a particular way from those who do not. In the United States it is common at civil functions like city council meetings or legislative gatherings for the attendees to recite the Pledge of Allegiance. The pledge is used widely in the United States, but isn’t used in other countries, because the citizens of those countries do not owe allegiance to the United States. The pledge is not what makes a person ‘American’, but it does represent a significant essential belief for those who are citizens.

The first recognizable versions of the creed were written in the fourth century A.D. The creed has changed slightly over the centuries, and has slight variations based on the denomination. Not every Christian denomination uses the creed, but in large part it is widely accepted in some form across the whole of Christianity.

The Apostles Creed has been used as a teaching and catechism tool for centuries and communicates the most fundamental truths of the Christian faith, the base beliefs that must be acknowledged and held to be a Christian. Some denominations have a formal catechism process which is how a new believer joins that specific denomination and becomes indoctrinated into their beliefs.

A person does not need to fully understand these beliefs to come to God and receive salvation, salvation is by faith, not by a doctrinal test! However, in continuing in that faith we all should come to understand these fundamental truths.

> *I believe in God, the Father almighty,  
> creator of heaven and earth.*
>
> *I believe in Jesus Christ, His only Son, our Lord.  
> He was conceived by the power of the Holy Spirit  
> and born of the Virgin Mary.  
> He suffered under Pontius Pilate,  
> was crucified, died, and was buried.  
> He descended to the dead.  
> On the third day He rose again.  
> He ascended into heaven,  
> and is seated at the right hand of the Father.  
> He will come again to judge the living and the dead.*
>
> *I believe in the Holy Spirit,  
> the holy catholic Church\*,  
> the communion of saints,  
> the forgiveness of sins,  
> the resurrection of the body,  
> and the life everlasting. Amen.*
>
> *Contemporary Version of the Apostles Creed, [<u>https://www.rca.org/about/theology/creeds-and-confessions/the-apostles-creed/</u>](https://www.rca.org/about/theology/creeds-and-confessions/the-apostles-creed/)*
>
> \*Meaning the universal Christian church-all believers in Jesus Christ

## Brief Explanation of the Apostles Creed

The fundamental truths in the Apostles Creed are numerous and quite deep, a full explanation would certainly take many whole volumes to explain. While this chapter is about Jesus, many of the topics cross over into the other members of the Godhead. This will be an abbreviated explanation of the creed, focusing only on the most central themes. Several of these topics are studied in greater depth across the chapters of this book.

#### *I believe in God, the Father almighty, creator of heaven and earth.*

This affirms belief in God as the all-powerful, sovereign Creator and Father. The title "Father" emphasizes God’s loving relationship with His creation, particularly His children through faith (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="1" data-verses="12" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 1:12</span>). As the Creator, He spoke the universe into existence, bringing order out of chaos (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Genesis" data-chapter="1" data-verses="1-3" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Genesis 1:1-3</span>). His almighty power is displayed in the vastness of creation, while His fatherly care is seen in His ongoing sustenance and providence. This foundational statement establishes God as the origin and ruler of all life, deserving worship and trust.

#### *I believe in Jesus Christ, His only Son, our Lord.*

Jesus Christ is the unique Son of God, eternally begotten, not created, and sharing in the divine nature (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="1" data-verses="1-2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 1:1-2</span>, 14). Jesus is the ‘Word’ since the beginning, and He became flesh and lived with us. As "Christ," He is the anointed Messiah, fulfilling the Old Testament promises of a Savior. The title "Lord" acknowledges His authority over all creation and His rightful position as ruler and King (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Philippians" data-chapter="2" data-verses="10-11" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Philippians 2:10-11</span>). He is not only Lord, but the Lord who saves (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="16" data-verses="31" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 16:31</span>).

Jesus is not **just** sent from God; He **is** part of God. Jesus abides in the Father and the Father abides in Jesus. The Gospel of John repeatedly establishes that Jesus and the Father are one (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="10" data-verses="30" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 10:30</span>), that Jesus did not act on His own accord (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="5" data-verses="19" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 5:19</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="12" data-verses="49-50" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 12:49-50</span>), and that everything He did was to glorify His Father (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="17" data-verses="4" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 17:4</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="14" data-verses="13" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 14:13</span>), and was done by the authority and power of His Father (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="14" data-verses="10" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 14:10</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="8" data-verses="28-29" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 8:28-29</span>).

#### *He was conceived by the power of the Holy Spirit and born of the Virgin Mary.*

This affirms the miraculous conception of Jesus through the Holy Spirit, underscoring His divine origin. Born of the Virgin Mary, Jesus fulfills the prophecy of <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Isaiah" data-chapter="7" data-verses="14" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Isaiah 7:14</span>, which foretold the virgin birth of the Messiah. This event marks the incarnation, where the eternal Word of God took on human flesh (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="1" data-verses="14" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 1:14</span>). By being both fully God and fully human, Jesus bridges the gap between God and humanity, enabling Him to be the perfect mediator and Savior.

Jesus was conceived by the seed of the Holy Spirit, and did not inherit a sinful nature that we, born of the seed of men, have. Jesus was in every way tempted and tested, but He was perfect and without sin. <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="4" data-verses="15" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 4:15</span>, <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="2 Corinthians" data-chapter="5" data-verses="21" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">2 Corinthians 5:21</span>, <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Peter" data-chapter="2" data-verses="22" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Peter 2:22</span>.

#### *He suffered under Pontius Pilate, was crucified, died, and was buried.*

Jesus’ suffering and death were real, historical events that occurred under the Roman governor Pontius Pilate, grounding the Christian faith in verifiable history. His crucifixion was a fulfillment of Old Testament prophecies, such as <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Isaiah" data-chapter="53" data-verses="5" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Isaiah 53:5</span>, which speaks of the Suffering Servant who would bear the sins of many. Jesus’ death was sacrificial, taking the penalty for sin on behalf of humanity (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Peter" data-chapter="2" data-verses="24" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Peter 2:24</span>). His burial confirms the reality of His death and prepares for the significance of the resurrection.

#### *He descended to the dead.*

After His death, Jesus descended to the realm of the dead, often referred to as *Hades* or *Sheol* in Scripture. This act demonstrates His full identification with humanity, even in death, and signifies His victory over sin, death, and the devil (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Peter" data-chapter="3" data-verses="18-20" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Peter 3:18-20</span>). By descending, He proclaimed His triumph and the completion of His redemptive work.

#### *On the third day He rose again.*

The resurrection of Jesus is the cornerstone of our Christian faith, confirming His divinity and the truth of His teachings. By rising from the dead, He defeated sin and death, providing the assurance of eternal life for believers (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="15" data-verses="17-22" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 15:17-22</span>). The resurrection also fulfills Scripture, including Jesus’ own predictions (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="16" data-verses="21" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 16:21</span>). This miraculous event transformed the despair of the disciples into bold faith, forming the foundation of the early Church's witness.

#### *He ascended into heaven, and is seated at the right hand of the Father.*

Jesus’ ascension marks His return to the Father’s presence in glory. Seated at the father’s right hand (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="1" data-verses="20" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 1:20</span>), He assumes the role of our eternal High Priest, interceding for believers (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="7" data-verses="25" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 7:25</span>). His position of authority also signifies His exaltation as King, reigning over all creation and directing the Church’s mission (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="1" data-verses="22" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 1:22</span>) to do greater works (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="14" data-verses="12" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 14:12</span>) and fulfilling the great commission He gave to us (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="28" data-verses="18-20" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 28:18-20</span>, <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Mark" data-chapter="16" data-verses="15-18" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Mark 16:15-18</span>). The ascension assures believers of His ongoing presence and power through the Holy Spirit.

#### *He will come again to judge the living and the dead.*

This points to the future return of Christ when He will bring final justice and restoration. Jesus will judge all people, rewarding the faithful and bringing justice to the unrepentant (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="2 Timothy" data-chapter="4" data-verses="1" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">2 Timothy 4:1</span>). His return will fulfill God’s ultimate plan for creation, ushering in His eternal Kingdom of righteousness and peace. This hope encourages believers to live faithfully and expectantly, knowing their labor in the Lord is not in vain (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="15" data-verses="58" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 15:58</span>).

#### *I believe in the Holy Spirit.*

The Holy Spirit, the third person of the Trinity, is God’s presence and power active in the world today. He convicts of sin, regenerates hearts, and empowers believers to live holy lives (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="16" data-verses="8-13" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 16:8-13</span>). The Spirit unites Christians as one body, the Church, and equips them with spiritual gifts for service (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="12" data-verses="4-11" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 12:4-11</span>). He is the Comforter, Advocate, and Guide, dwelling within believers as a seal of their eternal inheritance (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="1" data-verses="13-14" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 1:13-14</span>).

#### *The holy catholic Church, the communion of saints.*

The "holy catholic Church" refers to the universal body of Christ, comprising all believers across time and space. The Church is set apart for God’s purposes, called to reflect His holiness and share the gospel. The "communion of saints" emphasizes the unity of all Christians, living and dead, as members of Christ’s body (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="4" data-verses="4-6" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 4:4-6</span>). This fellowship transcends earthly divisions, demonstrating the shared hope and mission of believers.

Jesus is the head of the church. The church is referred to as both the bride of Christ (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="5" data-verses="25-27" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 5:25-27</span>) and the body of Christ (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Colossians" data-chapter="1" data-verses="18" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Colossians 1:18</span>).

#### *The forgiveness of sins.*

This highlights the heart of the gospel: through Jesus’ sacrificial death, God offers complete forgiveness for sins to all who repent and believe (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="1" data-verses="7" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 1:7</span>). This forgiveness restores the broken relationship between humanity and God, removing guilt and shame. It also calls believers to extend forgiveness to others, reflecting the grace they have received (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="6" data-verses="14-15" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 6:14-15</span>).

#### *The resurrection of the body.*

Like Jesus, we will experience a bodily resurrection at the end of time. This resurrection will transform mortal, perishable bodies into glorified, imperishable ones fit for eternal life (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="15" data-verses="42-44" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 15:42-44</span>). It underscores the Christian hope of restoration, not just for individuals, but for all creation, as God makes all things new (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Revelation" data-chapter="21" data-verses="4-5" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Revelation 21:4-5</span>).

#### *And the life everlasting. Amen.*

Eternal life with God is the ultimate promise of the Christian faith. This life begins now through a relationship with Christ and will be fully realized in the new heavens and new earth, where believers will dwell with God forever (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="3" data-verses="16" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 3:16</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Revelation" data-chapter="22" data-verses="1-5" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Revelation 22:1-5</span>). This closing affirmation reflects the joy and confidence of salvation, echoing the trust and hope expressed throughout the Creed.

## Conclusion

In the foundational truths expressed in the Apostles Creed, we see Jesus’ life and work as central to God’s plan for humanity. Each statement in the Creed captures key aspects of our belief in God, and who Jesus is-His divinity, humanity, mission, and ultimate triumph. These truths point to His unique role as the Mediator of the New Covenant. As Mediator He invites us to approach God with confidence, trusting in His perfect sacrifice and ongoing intercession. It calls us to worship Him as the rightful King and to follow His teachings as the ultimate Prophet. Ultimately, the study of Jesus leads to a deeper relationship with Him, transforming us into people who live in the light of His grace and truth.

Understanding who Jesus is and what He has accomplished is the foundation of the Christian faith. The many names, titles, and roles of Jesus illuminate different facets of His character and mission, providing a fuller picture of His love and authority.

Like our study of God, the scriptures are full of various names referring to Jesus. Each name represents a specific aspect of His total character, ministry, and role. These names were given to us to reveal an aspect of Jesus. For your personal study a reference table is included in the Appendix: *Table 12 - Names of Jesus.* Read through these names and study the scripture references.

## Personal Reflection Questions 

1.  Which role of Jesus-Prophet, Priest, or King-resonates most with you at this point in your faith journey? Why?

2.  How does the Apostles Creed help you better understand the foundational truths of Jesus’ mission and work?

3.  Reflect on one of the names of Jesus (e.g., Emmanuel, Lamb of God, Alpha, and Omega). How does this name reveal His character and impact your relationship with Him?

4.  Consider Jesus’ role as the Mediator of the New Covenant. How does this truth shape your understanding of salvation and your approach to God?

5.  In what ways do you see Jesus leading, interceding, or teaching you today? How can you respond to His work in these areas?

6.  If someone asked you to explain why Jesus is foundational to your faith, what would you say? How can this understanding strengthen your personal witness?

## Group Discussion Questions (20-30 Minutes)

1.  **Icebreaker Question:** What is one name or title of Jesus that has been especially meaningful to you? Why?

2.  **Exploring Scripture:** Read <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="1" data-verses="1-3" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 1:1-3</span>. How does this passage describe Jesus’ role as the ultimate Prophet? How does this apply to us today?

3.  **Prophet, Priest, King:** What does it mean for Jesus to fulfill all three roles? How do these roles address humanity’s greatest needs?

4.  **Our Foundation:** What are practical ways we can build our lives on Jesus as the foundation of our faith? How can this affect our relationships, work, or ministry?

5.  **What if:** Why is it important that Jesus fulfills all three roles-Prophet, Priest, and King? What would be incomplete if one of these roles were missing?

# Chapter 8 - Holy Spirit-Comforter, Guide, and Power

The Holy Spirit is not a new spirit but is of God and has been with God since the very beginning. In <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Genesis" data-chapter="1" data-verses="2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Genesis 1:2</span> the Spirit of God is hovering on the waters during creation. In <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Nehemiah" data-chapter="9" data-verses="20" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Nehemiah 9:20</span>, the scriptures say that it was the Spirit who instructed the people in the wilderness. It was by this same Holy Spirit that God anointed the kings, judges, and prophets in the Old Testament. In <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Joel" data-chapter="2" data-verses="28-29" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Joel 2:28-29</span> the Spirit is promised to be poured out on all mankind. It was by this same Holy Spirit that God raised Jesus from the dead, and now dwells in us (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="8" data-verses="11" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 8:11</span>). There is only one Holy Spirit, and He has always been the same. How He works with people has looked different at different times, but His heart has never changed.

Jesus gave His disciples, and all of us, the powerful promise of the Holy Spirit.


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="John" data-chapter="14" data-verses="16-18" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>John 14:16-18</u>** (NKJV) **16** *And I will pray the Father, and He will give you another Helper, that He may abide with you forever-**17** the Spirit of truth, whom the world cannot receive, because it neither sees Him nor knows Him; but you know Him, for He dwells with you and will be in you.* ***18** I will not leave you orphans; I will come to you.*

</span>


While Jesus walked the earth in His physical body, He was limited as we are, His body needed the same things our bodies need: rest, water, food, sleep, etc. This limitation not only impacted Jesus, but impacted His followers, as He could only be in one place at a time. Oftentimes as Jesus traveled, He was pressed about by a huge crowd, they would try to call out to Him and touch Him. Jesus often needed to spend time with His heavenly Father to recharge and pray.

For the work of the Father to be multiplied Jesus needed to raise up disciples, and for them to carry on this work. Jesus knew that a crucial aspect of His ministry on the earth depended upon Him connecting with and abiding with His Father. In fact, Jesus said He could do nothing unless His Father did it through Him and showed Him to do it (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="5" data-verses="19" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 5:19</span>). God in His wisdom sent His Holy Spirit to dwell within us, to lead us and guide us, empowering us to do the work of the Father.

## When was the Holy Spirit Sent to All Believers?

Jesus said:


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="John" data-chapter="16" data-verses="7" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>John 16:7</u>** (NKJV) *Nevertheless I tell you the truth. It is to your advantage that I go away; for if I do not go away, the Helper will not come to you; but if I depart, I will send Him to you.*

</span>


After Jesus was crucified, His death made it possible for God’s Spirit to dwell inside us. Until that point, God's tangible Spirit was mostly contained behind a thick veil in the temple. From time to time, select individuals were chosen by God and anointed (consecrated and set apart for unique service), and received the Holy Spirit upon them. God’s anointing and the Spirit upon them, was special, temporary, and conditional. David prayed after his sin with Bathsheba ‘*Take not your Holy Spirit from me!*’ (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Psalm" data-chapter="51" data-verses="11" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Psalm 51:11</span>), because David had seen the Spirit lift off King Saul for his acts of disobedience.

We (Mankind) could not receive the Holy Spirit inside us because of our sinful nature, but when Jesus dealt with the sin issue through His sacrifice, it made a way through faith that we might become children and heirs of God (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="8" data-verses="17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 8:17</span>).

In John 20, after His resurrection, Jesus appears to His disciples in the middle of a locked room and breathes on them saying ‘*Receive the Holy Spirit*’ (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="20" data-verses="22" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 20:22</span>). The theology is somewhat debated on precisely what occurred at that moment, but most Pentecostal interpretations view this as the disciples receiving the Spirit of God inside them, signifying the new birth, being born-again. This was not, however when they received the Spirit of God upon them, empowering them with boldness for ministry. This was when they received the Holy Spirit within them, allowing them to abide and commune with God. This will be discussed at greater depth later in this chapter as well as the chapters on sanctification and baptism.

Later at the conclusion of the 40-day period between resurrection and ascension (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="1" data-verses="3" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 1:3</span>), Jesus gives the promise of the Holy Spirit:


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="1" data-verses="4-9" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Acts 1:4-9</u>** (NKJV)
>
> **4** *And being assembled together with them, He commanded them not to depart from Jerusalem, but to wait for the Promise of the Father, "which," He said, "you have heard from Me;*
>
> ***5** for John truly baptized with water, but you shall be baptized with the Holy Spirit not many days from now."*
>
> ***6** Therefore, when they had come together, they asked Him, saying, "Lord, will You at this time restore the kingdom to Israel?"*
>
> ***7** And He said to them, "It is not for you to know times or seasons which the Father has put in His own authority.*
>
> ***8** But you shall receive power when the Holy Spirit has come upon you; and you shall be witnesses to Me in Jerusalem, and in all Judea and Samaria, and to the end of the earth."*
>
> ***9** Now when He had spoken these things, while they watched, He was taken up, and a cloud received Him out of their sight.*

</span>


The disciples were to wait for the promise of the Spirit poured out, and that this was to empower them to be witnesses to the ends of the earth. The Holy Spirit is integral to God’s plan for humanity, and is essential to us as believers, for completing our mission, and to the world.

## Getting to Know the Holy Spirit

The Holy Spirit is first and foremost, from and of God. The scriptures refer to the Spirit as the Spirit of God, or the Spirit of the Lord, or the Spirit of Christ, while these highlight different aspects, they are all one in the same. The Holy Spirit, being fully God, is also eternal and is holy (blameless, innocent, pure, not profane). Some translations refer to the Holy Spirit as the Holy Ghost.

The word spirit in Greek is ***Pneuma** (NYOO-mah -* **G4151***),* it means breath, or wind. The Hebrew word for spirit means the same. Breath is significant as we need it in constant supply to live. It was God's breath that He blew into Adam to bring Him to life, likewise it was Jesus' breath that He breathed last before He died on the cross. Without breath there is no life. Breath like wind can be felt and experienced but are not as easily observed as other ‘elements’ water, fire, earth.

There are several other core adjectives to describe the Holy Spirit. Each of these adjectives are used in conjunction with ‘the Spirit of’, meaning that these properties are vital to the definition of the Spirit. Example, the *Spirit of Truth* or the *Spirit of Life*.

The Spirit of:

- **Truth** - <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="14" data-verses="17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 14:17</span>, <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="16" data-verses="13" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 16:13</span> Refers to the Spirit's role in guiding believers into all truth, revealing God's will and the reality of Christ.

- **Life** - <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="8" data-verses="2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 8:2</span>, <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Galatians" data-chapter="6" data-verses="8" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Galatians 6:8</span> Highlights the Spirit's role in imparting spiritual and eternal life to believers, freeing them from the law of sin and death.

- **Grace** - <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="10" data-verses="29" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 10:29</span>, <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Zechariah" data-chapter="12" data-verses="10" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Zechariah 12:10</span> Reflects the Spirit's work in dispensing God's favor, forgiveness, and undeserved kindness to humanity.

- **Glory** - <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Peter" data-chapter="4" data-verses="14" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Peter 4:14</span> Indicates the Spirit's role in revealing and manifesting God's glory, particularly in times of suffering and persecution.

- **Adoption** - <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="8" data-verses="15" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 8:15</span>, <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Galatians" data-chapter="4" data-verses="5" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Galatians 4:5</span> Refers to the Spirit's work in making believers children of God, granting them the status of sons and daughters.

- **Holiness** - <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="1" data-verses="4" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 1:4</span> Points to the Spirit's sanctifying work, setting apart believers for God's purposes.

- **Power** - <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="2 Timothy" data-chapter="1" data-verses="7" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">2 Timothy 1:7</span>, <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="1" data-verses="8" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 1:8</span> Describes the Spirit's empowerment of believers for boldness, ministry, and the ability to live out their faith effectively.

In all these attributes the Holy Spirit is also properly recognized as the Helper or Comforter. Jesus refers to the Holy Spirit as the coming helper, He uses the Greek word ***Paraklētos** (*pah-RAH-klay-tos - **G3875**), which means advocate, helper, called alongside.

An advocate speaks on another's behalf, like a defense attorney. They intercede and help the person they are charged with.

The helper helps. They help empower the work, but their job is primarily to aid the work. The helper is not solely responsible for performing the work, but they come alongside to help accomplish it.

The Holy Spirit has several distinct purposes and roles in His ministry. His work is inward (in the hearts and lives of believers), upwards (in service and recognition of God), and outwards (to our mission field, the world).

## Roles of the Holy Spirit-Inward, Upward, Outward

### Inward-The Holy Spirit Unites Us With Jesus

Before Jesus did any miracles or launched His public ministry, He was baptized by John the Baptist and God's Spirit descended upon him, and a voice from heaven proclaimed, ‘This is my beloved Son in whom I am well pleased’ (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="3" data-verses="17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 3:17</span>).

When we accept Jesus by faith, we receive salvation and the seal of the Holy Spirit.


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="1" data-verses="13-14" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Ephesians 1:13-14</u>** (NKJV) ***13** In Him you also trusted, after you heard the word of truth, the gospel of your salvation; in whom also, having believed, you were sealed with the Holy Spirit of promise, **14** who is the guarantee of our inheritance until the redemption of the purchased possession, to the praise of His glory.*

</span>


This seal is not metaphorical but is literal. It serves as a sign of our redemption and is a down payment on the full inheritance we receive as children of God. Romans 8 describes that through the new birth we receive the same exact Spirit of God that raised Christ from the dead in our mortal bodies and we become the literal temple of the Holy Spirit (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="2 Corinthians" data-chapter="6" data-verses="16" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">2 Corinthians 6:16</span>).

This receiving of God's Spirit transforms us, and it causes rivers of living water to flow from us (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="7" data-verses="38" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 7:38</span>). This renewal is immediate and complete but continues through our entire lives. The river of the Holy Spirit should never stop flowing in our lives!

Through salvation and the indwelling of the Holy Spirit we can abide in Christ and He in us. God’s Spirit is no longer stuck on the outside but can dwell deeply with us. We can walk in communion and harmony with God from now through eternal life.

There is much more to say about the inward work of the Holy Spirit and His role on our sanctification, but that will be discussed in the chapter on sanctification.

### Upward-The Holy Spirit Testifies of Jesus

The Holy Spirit is from God and is of God. As Jesus said He did nothing except the father show Him (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="5" data-verses="19" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 5:19</span>), the Holy Spirit too does nothing unless it's directed from the father. Because the Holy Spirit is the helper, He does not work on His own accord, but works in perfect harmony with the father.

The Spirit testifies of Jesus (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="15" data-verses="26" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 15:26</span>), glorifies Jesus, and announces and declares what Jesus has for us (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="16" data-verses="14" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 16:14</span>).

The Holy Spirit will testify of Jesus, glorify him, teach, and bring to our remembrance the teaching of Jesus (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="14" data-verses="26" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 14:26</span>), and guide us into all truth (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="16" data-verses="13" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 16:13</span>). Jesus told His disciples that He had much more to teach them, but they could not yet bear it, but that the Holy Spirit would guide them into all truth. This is another nuanced point in theology. These teachings that Jesus needed His disciples to know were communicated in several ways. Jesus taught them in person both before his crucifixion, as well as after the resurrection and before His ascension.

Jesus also promised that the Spirit of Truth, that was poured out upon them on the day of Pentecost would lead them into all truth through divine revelation. Is there still divine revelation today? **Certainly yes!** The gifts of the Holy Spirit, including prophecy and words of knowledge and wisdom have not yet ceased! However, no prophecy or word from a spirit is of private interpretation (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="2 Peter" data-chapter="1" data-verses="20" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">2 Peter 1:20</span>). This is crucial. Some people will claim in error they are listening to the Holy Spirit, but their actions and words do not testify of Jesus. <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 John" data-chapter="4" data-verses="3" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 John 4:3</span> warns against any Spirit that does not testify of Jesus, saying that spirit is of the antichrist. This bears repeating, if any spirit strays from the teaching of Christ it is not the Holy Spirit, but a profane one!


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="1 John" data-chapter="4" data-verses="1" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>1 John 4:1</u>** (NKJV) *Beloved, do not believe every spirit, but test the spirits, whether they are of God; because many false prophets have gone out into the world.*

</span>


Throughout Scripture, we see examples of claims made in error, often by false prophets or well-meaning individuals who spoke contrary to God’s will. For instance, Jeremiah warned against prophets who spoke visions of their own minds, claiming peace while destruction loomed (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Jeremiah" data-chapter="23" data-verses="16-17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Jeremiah 23:16-17</span>). Similarly, in <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="16" data-verses="16-18" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 16:16-18</span>, a slave girl’s words about Paul and Silas were accurate but inspired by a demonic spirit, not the Holy Spirit. These examples remind us that even words that seem true can originate from ungodly sources.

Every claim of ‘God told me!’ or a ‘new’ teaching should be measured against the biblical tests of truth. To discern rightly, we must ask:

- Does this align with Scripture?

- Does this exalt Jesus Christ?

- Does this produce godly fruit?

By applying these tests, we can distinguish the true guidance of the Holy Spirit from errors or deception.

God is in the habit of providing us with ample & abundant wisdom and direction (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="James" data-chapter="1" data-verses="5" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">James 1:5</span> & <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Jeremiah" data-chapter="33" data-verses="3" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Jeremiah 33:3</span>). God’s divine wisdom will not violate the scriptures, but they will go beyond the scope of the scriptures. His *entolē* *(*commandments*)* are immediate prescriptions to be obeyed, they are *rhema* words (as discussed in the chapter on faith).

This wisdom and understanding and direction are likely not word for word in the scriptures. As an example, the scriptures give great general direction on what to look for in a spouse, but don’t give specific prescriptions on the exact person. God does promise to direct our path, lead us into all truth, and we can take comfort knowing that this wisdom and leading from the Holy Spirit will never violate any teaching of scripture.

### Outward-The Holy Spirit Empowers the Mission

Jesus gave us His mission, the Great Commission:


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="28" data-verses="18-20" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Matthew 28:18-20</u>** (NKJV) ***18** And Jesus came and spoke to them, saying, "All authority has been given to Me in heaven and on earth.* ***19** Go therefore and make disciples of all the nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit,* ***20** teaching them to observe all things that I have commanded you; and lo, I am with you always, even to the end of the age." Amen.*

</span>


The Holy Spirit has an integral part in the mission, convicting the world of sin, righteousness, and judgement:


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="John" data-chapter="16" data-verses="8-11" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>John 16:8-11</u>** (NKJV) **8** A*nd when He has come, He will convict the world of sin, and of righteousness, and of judgment:* ***9** of sin, because they do not believe in Me;* ***10** of righteousness, because I go to My Father and you see Me no more;* ***11** of judgment, because the ruler of this world is judged.*

</span>


Diving deeper, the Spirit brings conviction of these three truths:

1.  **Conviction of sin**: Revealing the reality of human rebellion against God, drawing men’s attention to the depravity of their own lives.

2.  **Conviction of righteousness**: Demonstrating Jesus’ perfect fulfillment of the Father’s will, and that Jesus' sacrifice provided the one-time atonement needed for forgiveness of sins.

3.  **Conviction of judgment**: Affirming the defeat of Satan, the certainty of God’s justice, and that all will stand a judgement for their life's work and deeds.

The Holy Spirit also empowers the mission. The Holy Spirit gives believers the power to be bold witnesses in the face of persecution to share the gospel. Boldness does not mean loudness. It means to have courage despite fear. Power is force, strength, and ability. The Holy Spirit gives the strength and ability to be a witness of the Gospel despite threats and the fear of punishment.


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="1" data-verses="8" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Acts 1:8</u>** *(NKJV) But you shall receive power when the Holy Spirit has come upon you; and you shall be witnesses to Me in Jerusalem, and in all Judea and Samaria, and to the end of the earth.*

</span>


The entire book of Acts is full of stories and accounts that have this general cycle:

1.  **Prayer**: Believers ask for boldness and God’s intervention.

2.  **Empowerment**: The Holy Spirit fills them with courage, power, and guidance.

3.  **Proclamation**: They boldly declare the gospel, often with signs and wonders and miracles.

4.  **Opposition**: Resistance arises, leading to persecution or challenges. Sometimes believers were martyred, other times imprisoned and or beaten.

5.  **Renewal**: The church returns to prayer, seeking fresh empowerment, and the cycle continues.

The New Testament church has largely grown in the face of determined opposition. Around the world the church suffers violence at the hands of oppressors and wicked rules intent to suppress the knowledge of the truth of Jesus.

God knew that this opposition would occur, Jesus prophesied concerning it:


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Luke" data-chapter="12" data-verses="11-12" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Luke 12:11-12</u>** (NKJV) **11** *"Now when they bring you to the synagogues and magistrates and authorities, do not worry about how or what you should answer, or what you should say. **12** For the Holy Spirit will teach you in that very hour what you ought to say."*

</span>


Do not fear! Jesus promised us that:


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="fragment" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="16" data-verses="18" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Matthew 16:18</u>** (NKJV) *… I \[Jesus\] will build My church, and the gates of Hades shall not prevail against it.*

</span>


This is an amazing and comforting promise. Gates are fixed defensive armaments. When Jesus says that the *gates of hell shall not prevail*, He is painting the picture of a conquering army that knocks down the gates and captures the city!

The Holy Spirit brings supernatural boldness to proclaim the witness of the Gospel in the face of persecution. The outcome of this empowerment can be described, but it's best experienced. It’s a supernatural (greater than human) boldness that motivates believers to share. This boldness of preaching is often accompanied by supernatural acts of demonstration of the Spirit and of power (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="2" data-verses="1-5" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 2:1-5</span>). People are healed physically and emotionally, the dead raised, those tormented by evil spirits are set free, people with hard hearts turn and repent.

Jesus said these works and greater would we do! (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="14" data-verses="12" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 14:12</span>). These collective works are supernatural, divine, and desperately needed in a world tearing itself apart, miserable, full of pain, waiting for the mature sons of God to be made clearly manifest! (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="8" data-verses="22-23" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 8:22-23</span>)

Jesus' chosen instrument for change on the earth is His church, empowered, emboldened, and directed by the Holy Spirit.

## Building a Relationship with the Holy Spirit

Every relationship takes time and experience to develop deeply. The Holy Spirit is our advocate, our comforter, our helper, and our guide. He empowers us and sanctifies us.

Start to think of the Holy Spirit as you would a person. In our relationship we can both work to build it or do things that weaken our relationship. He can be ignored, He can be quenched, He can be blasphemed, or He can be listened to, honored, and obeyed.

Don’t ignore the voice of the Holy Spirit. He is always speaking to us and the church. If you don’t yet know how to distinguish the voice of the Holy Spirit, it takes time, willingness, and effort (like all healthy and beneficial relationships). But don’t worry, you already have built into you all the necessary parts needed to start to develop a personal relationship with the Holy Spirit.

The quickest and most reliable ways to build an effective relationship with the Holy Spirit is to

1.  Call out to Him **individually** in prayer, pause and wait for Him to speak.

2.  Call out to Him as part of a **group** of people who know Him (the church), pause, and wait for Him to speak.

I use the phrase ‘call out’ to mean literally, with your mouth, or however you speak (with your hands if you cannot speak aloud with your mouth). Why speak? A few reasons:

1.  We are speaking spirits made in God's image. God speaks, and so do we.

2.  We use speaking as the primary way to communicate. You can also write down what you want to say (journaling) and speak it aloud.

3.  Speaking is an intentional act, and indicates to the party you are talking to, that you’d like to engage them in a conversation.

#### What should you say?

If you’ve never talked to God, try introducing yourself. Sure, it will feel a bit weird, but that discomfort is okay and temporary. Along with introducing yourself, tell God that you want to talk to Him and that you want Him to talk back to you.

Consider asking Him a question. It doesn’t have to be a deep theological question; it can start simple. Can you show me Your love for me? Why do I have a certain feeling? What would You like to tell me?

#### Then what?

Well in all effective relationships you speak for a bit, then you pause and listen. God will speak.

God speaks in many ways, but He wants to do it personally, and internally. It will likely not be an audible voice (like if your best friend was to say your name aloud) but it will be an internal voice that speaks to your heart and mind. Elijah refers to this as the ‘still small voice’ (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Kings" data-chapter="19" data-verses="11-13" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Kings 19:11-13</span>).

The most common ways that I've personally witnessed God talk to people, and I've been blessed to help dozens of people learn how to talk to God directly, is some combination of the following:

- A knowing - the person talking to God gets an immediate understanding that helps them.

- A picture - also known in the scriptures as a vision or a dream (a vision is when you are awake, a dream is when you are asleep), God will give you a picture in your mind. Perhaps it’s a memory, or place you’ve been or know? Explore the picture, what do you see? What do you feel as a result? What’s happening?

- An inner voice - like an audible voice, but one that only you can hear, and spoken into your heart and mind.

#### What will God say?

Not sure! But that’s the fun in it. I can say that He is NOT the spirit of fear, but of perfect love and that it will never violate the teaching of the scriptures. E.g., God will never tell you to commit an extra-marital affair.

Now what?

Keep the dialogue going! It’s really that simple.

A few good books I’ve read and will personally recommend to help you learn to talk to God are:

- *Genuine Christianity* by Larry Low ([<u>www.larrylow.com</u>](http://www.larrylow.com))

- *Good Morning Holy Spirit* by Benny Hinn ([<u>www.bennyhinn.org</u>](http://www.bennyhinn.org))

### Why build a relationship?

The Holy Spirit is the Helper. The Helper helps us! The work goes much more smoothly if we have a shared language, and relationship with Him. Can you imagine trying to work with someone in your job if you had no relationship, did not speak the same language, and couldn't communicate?!

We serve a God who wants to communicate with us. Jesus said that His sheep know His voice (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="10" data-verses="27" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 10:27</span>), we have built into us all already the natural abilities we need, but we may lack experienced proficiency.

Speaking from personal experience, a personal and active relationship with the Holy Spirit is one of the best things about being a Christian. His presence brings us peace, comfort, and direction, but only if you know how to seek, listen, and obey Him.

Develop a sensitivity with the Holy Spirit. Like many married people over time, you can communicate with your spouse in a unique way and sometimes just through a look. The look alone tells you everything you need to know about what just happened and what's going to happen. Build that type of sensitivity and familiarity with the Holy Spirit. Include Him in your whole life, this is a wonderful part of your relationship with Him that will provide so much depth and richness to your walk of faith.

The Holy Spirit has come to lead us and guide us, to empower us to be God’s people, and to see the people of the Earth radically transformed, delivered from the kingdom of darkness into the kingdom of light. Getting to know him, building trust with Him is essential to everyday life and to accomplish the work that Jesus has for us to do.

Our discussion on the work of the Holy Spirit is not finished in this chapter. His work is discussed at length across the remaining chapters of this book, especially the chapters on sanctification and baptism.

## Personal Reflection Questions 

1.  How have you experienced the Holy Spirit as your Comforter, Guide, or source of power? Which role resonates most with your current season of life?

2.  Reflect on the difference between the Holy Spirit being “within” (inward work) and “upon” (outward work). How does this distinction impact your understanding of His work in your life?

3.  What steps can you take to cultivate a deeper relationship with the Holy Spirit and recognize His voice more clearly?

4.  We are to rely on the Holy Spirit’s power to step out in boldness and fulfill the mission God has given us. What specific mission and focus has God given you?

5.  The Holy Spirit leads us into truth and discernment. Are there areas of your life where you need clarity or wisdom? How can you invite Him to guide you?

## Group Discussion Questions (20-30 Minutes)

1.  **Icebreaker Question:** “When you think of the Holy Spirit, what is the first word or image that comes to mind? Why?”

2.  **Exploring Scripture:** Read <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="14" data-verses="26" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 14:26</span>. How does the Holy Spirit’s role as Teacher and Helper encourage you in your faith journey?

3.  **Holy Spirit Within vs. Upon:** Discuss the difference between the Holy Spirit being “within” for transformation and “upon” for empowerment. Why are both aspects essential for a believer?
