4.  **Practical Guidance:** What are practical ways we can position ourselves to hear the Holy Spirit’s voice and follow His leading in daily decisions?

5.  **Reflection**: Think of a time when you felt empowered by the Holy Spirit to act. How did that experience impact your faith and the people around you?

# Chapter 9 - The Church & Our Role in It

The church is how God shows Himself on earth-through His people, doing His work. We are called to be His body, we are His hands and feet to accomplish His work and purpose. Without our efforts the harvest will remain in the field and will go un-reaped.

## Who is the Church?

The church, ***Ekklesia** (eh-klee-SEE-ah - **G1577**),* is derived from two Greek words:

- **"Ek"**: "Out of."

- **"Kaleo"**: "To call."

Together, **Ekklesia** literally means "called out" or "called out assembly." In ancient Greek city-states, it referred to a gathering of citizens summoned for a specific purpose, such as legislative or judicial meetings. It was a secular term denoting an assembly of people.

In the New Testament**,** it is used primarily to refer to the community of believers in Jesus Christ. It can denote:

- **The Local Church**: A specific gathering of believers in a particular location (e.g., the church at Corinth, Philippi, etc.).


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="1" data-verses="2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>1 Corinthians 1:2</u>** (NKJV) "*To the church of God which is in Corinth.*"

</span>


- **The Universal Church**: The collective body of all believers across time and space.


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="fragment" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="16" data-verses="18" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Matthew 16:18</u>** (NKJV) …*I \[Jesus\] will build My church, and the gates of Hades shall not prevail against it.*

</span>


The church is the people, not the building or location they meet. **Called-Out Ones**: Reflects the idea that Christians are called out of the world and set apart for God's purposes (e.g., <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Peter" data-chapter="2" data-verses="9" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Peter 2:9</span>) for a specific mission and purpose (the great commission). The church is technically any two or more believers meeting together (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="18" data-verses="20" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 18:20</span>). Typically, the church is organized into regular meetings, with perhaps a set location and time, but wherever two or more people meet in Jesus’ name, that's where the church is!

The church was established by Jesus and increased in the 1st century A.D. by the work of the disciples who followed him. The church we have and experience today in all its local bodies across the Earth is a direct result of the original work of Christ and His apostles. As believers we are to be committed to a local church for fellowship, provision, equipping for ministry, and to do good works!

To help us understand what the church is, the Scriptures gives us powerful pictures that show what it’s meant to be.

## Understanding the Church

There are several core metaphorical images used to depict attributes of the church:

1.  The Body

2.  The Bride

3.  The Temple

4.  The Family

5.  The Flock

6.  The Vine and Branches

Each analogy highlights a unique aspect of the church, yet together, they reveal the church's identity as a unified and diverse community under Christ's leadership.

### The Body

The church is often described as **the Body of Christ**, emphasizing both its unity and diversity. With Christ as the head, the church functions as a living organism where every believer plays an essential and potentially diverse role. Each member is uniquely gifted and contributes to the health and mission of the body according to their own function. This image highlights the interdependence of believers, specifically that the body must be connected to function, and that our function is collaborative not competitive. When every part works together, the body grows and thrives in unity and love (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="12" data-verses="12-27" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 12:12-27</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="4" data-verses="15-16" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 4:15-16</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Colossians" data-chapter="1" data-verses="18" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Colossians 1:18</span>).

### The Bride

The church is described as **the Bride of Christ**, this reflects an intimate and loving relationship with Jesus. Just as a bride is cherished and made ready for her husband, the church is sanctified and purified by Christ’s sacrificial love. This metaphor captures the devotion and faithfulness expected of the church, as well as the deep affection Christ has for His people. The imagery also points to the ultimate union between Christ and the church, celebrated in the marriage supper of the Lamb, where the church is presented in glory without spot or blemish (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="5" data-verses="25-27" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 5:25-27</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Revelation" data-chapter="19" data-verses="7-9" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Revelation 19:7-9</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Revelation" data-chapter="21" data-verses="2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Revelation 21:2</span>).

### The Temple

The church is described as **the Temple of God**, the dwelling place of His Spirit. Believers are described as living stones being built together on the foundation of the apostles and prophets, with Christ Himself as the cornerstone. This holy structure signifies God’s presence among His people and their role as a community of worship and prayer. The temple imagery underscores the importance of holiness and the church’s collective purpose in glorifying God (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="3" data-verses="16-17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 3:16-17</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="2" data-verses="19-22" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 2:19-22</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Peter" data-chapter="2" data-verses="4-5" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Peter 2:4-5</span>).

### The Family

The church is described as **the Family of God**, emphasizing relational unity and care among its members. Through the Spirit of adoption, believers are made children of God and members of His household. This familial imagery highlights the love, belonging, and shared inheritance believers have in Christ. It also reflects the call to treat one another with compassion and humility as brothers and sisters in God’s family (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Galatians" data-chapter="6" data-verses="10" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Galatians 6:10</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="2" data-verses="19" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 2:19</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="8" data-verses="15-17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 8:15-17</span>). Furthermore, through the family image it depicts how the members of the church are to become mature, that they would be taught and nourished by spiritual mothers and fathers (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Timothy" data-chapter="5" data-verses="1-2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Timothy 5:1-2</span>), that we could go from infants drinking the pure milk of the Word, to full grown adults able to eat the strong meat of the Word (Hebrews 5).

### The Flock

The church is described as **the Flock**, sheep under the care of Jesus, the Good Shepherd. This image conveys the church’s dependence on Christ for guidance, sustenance, and protection. It also emphasizes the responsibility of church leaders to faithfully shepherd God’s flock as under shepherds, mirroring Christ’s example of love and care. The flock imagery speaks to trust, obedience, and the close relationship between the Great Shepherd and His sheep (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="10" data-verses="14-16" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 10:14-16</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Peter" data-chapter="5" data-verses="2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Peter 5:2</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="20" data-verses="28" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 20:28</span>).

### The Vine and Branches

The church is described as **the Vine and Branches**, illustrating the essential connection between believers and Christ. Jesus is the true vine, and believers are the branches that must remain in Him to bear fruit. This metaphor highlights dependence on Christ for spiritual vitality, growth, and productivity. Without Him, believers can do nothing, but by abiding in Him, they glorify God and fulfill their purpose. Jesus will prune so that He can bear more fruit in our lives (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="15" data-verses="1-5" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 15:1-5</span>).

## Roles of the Church

The church’s mission, like the Holy Spirit's role, can be understood through three dimensions: upward, inward, and outward, each reflecting a unique aspect of its calling in God’s plan. These dimensions guide the church in fulfilling its role as a worshiping, edifying, and missional community.

### Upward: Worship and Glorifying God 

The church’s first purpose is to worship and glorify God. Corporate worship, where believers gather to exalt God together, is a visible reflection of His glory and a response to His greatness. As the psalmist declares, “*Worship the Lord in the splendor of holiness; tremble before him, all the earth*” (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Psalm" data-chapter="96" data-verses="9" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Psalm 96:9</span>). Beyond corporate worship, the church is called to live lives of obedience and sacrifice, offering themselves as “living sacrifices” as an act of spiritual worship (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="12" data-verses="1-2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 12:1-2</span>). This upward focus keeps the church centered on God’s majesty and aligns its priorities with His will.

### Inward: Building Up the Body 

The Great Commission commands us to ‘*teach them to observe’*, this command goes much further than formal teaching methods like this book. Discipleship making (discussed further in the chapter on discipleship) is forming the follower into the likeness of the discipline, like how a parent raises their child. Good parents don’t hand a five-year-old an encyclopedia and challenge them to figure it out! Rather they model, demonstrate, correct, guide, nurture, love, care, and shepherd.

Parents sacrifice for their children because they love them, because they are part of the same body, because they share the same blood. As members of the body and family of Christ, we share the same body, we share the same blood. Communion reminds us through its symbolism that we have been joined to one another through Christ’s sacrifice. The act of communion is to be a point of unity, reminding us that we all have need of redemption, and that salvation is only from God. In this unity we see that no part of the body is greater than another except for our head, Christ.

### Outward: Reaching the World 

The most well-known part of the church’s mission extends outward to reach the world with the gospel. Jesus’ Great Commission calls His followers to evangelize and make disciples of all nations (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="28" data-verses="19-20" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 28:19-20</span>). Empowered by the Holy Spirit, the church is to be His witnesses to the ends of the earth (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="1" data-verses="8" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 1:8</span>).

Jesus is the light of the world (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="8" data-verses="12" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 8:12</span>). Light shines in all directions outward from the source. His light shines through His manifestations: the Word, His Spirit, and us, His body. We are the light of the world; our lives we are to radiate His light in our darkened worlds and demonstrate good works that glorify God (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="5" data-verses="14-16" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 5:14-16</span>). Practically this means the church is called to advocate for justice and mercy, both spiritual and physical. As James says, our pure worship is to care for the vulnerable, the fatherless, the widow (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="James" data-chapter="1" data-verses="27" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">James 1:27</span>).

## The Leadership of the Church

Across Christendom there are a wide variety of denominations, meetings, gatherings, and assemblies. There are very few universal rules to the leadership of the church, but the rules we do have are very significant:

1.  Jesus is the singular head of the Church. Everyone serves Him (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Colossians" data-chapter="1" data-verses="18" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Colossians 1:18</span>).

2.  There are high standards for those who lead, they are worthy of double honor, for they will receive double judgement (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Timothy" data-chapter="5" data-verses="17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Timothy 5:17</span>).

    1.  They should be mature, not puffed up, responsible (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Timothy" data-chapter="3" data-verses="1-13" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Timothy 3:1-13</span>, <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Titus" data-chapter="1" data-verses="5-9" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Titus 1:5-9</span>).

    2.  They must be servants of all (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Mark" data-chapter="10" data-verses="42-45" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Mark 10:42-45</span>).

    3.  They must commit themselves to the Word and to prayer (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="6" data-verses="4" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 6:4</span>).

    4.  They are responsible for equipping the body for the work of ministry.

3.  There are no greater or lesser members of the body. Scriptures supporting this are:

    1.  There should be no partiality between rich and poor (James 2).

    2.  There is no division between Jew and Gentile, slave, or freedman, male or female (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Galatians" data-chapter="3" data-verses="27-28" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Galatians 3:27-28</span>).

The structure of the Church, beyond the leadership rules, is important but much of it is situational and a matter of tradition rather than universally dictated. “<u>That's the way we’ve always done it!</u>” is a popular statement; if you go back far enough, there was usually a good reason for why they did it that way. This is generally neither good nor bad, but is a matter of taste, needs, and availability.

A useful example of tradition being the foundation for structure is family dinner time. The most important part of family dinner time is that everyone gets fed and that the meal is nourishing. The variations are endless: what they eat, how they eat, how they season their food, etc. The various combinations are generally acceptable and largely a matter of taste and preference.

A church with 10,000 members has different needs and abilities than a church of 50. The pattern setup in the book of Acts and through the New Testament canon was the model of **elders** and **deacons**. While leadership structures can look different between churches and denominations, ultimately this model represents the minimally viable structure of leadership for the church. Let’s take a look at these leadership positions and how they are selected.

### Elders (Overseers)

Role and Responsibilities:

- **Spiritual Leadership** - Provide oversight, guidance, and pastoral care for the church (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="20" data-verses="28" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 20:28</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Peter" data-chapter="5" data-verses="2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Peter 5:2</span>).

- **Teaching and Preaching** - Responsible for teaching sound doctrine and protecting against false teaching (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Timothy" data-chapter="5" data-verses="17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Timothy 5:17</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Titus" data-chapter="1" data-verses="9" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Titus 1:9</span>).

- **Shepherding the Flock** - Care for the spiritual needs of the congregation, modeling humility and service (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Peter" data-chapter="5" data-verses="2-3" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Peter 5:2-3</span>).

- **Guarding the Church** - Serve as watchmen, defending the church from false doctrine and division (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="20" data-verses="29-31" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 20:29-31</span>).

- **Equipping the Saints** - Train and equip believers for works of ministry and discipleship (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="4" data-verses="11-12" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 4:11-12</span>).

Selection Process:

- **Appointed in Every Church** - Elders were appointed by the apostles or their representatives, often through prayer and fasting (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="14" data-verses="23" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 14:23</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Titus" data-chapter="1" data-verses="5" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Titus 1:5</span>).

- **Confirmed by the Holy Spirit** - Their appointment was seen as Spirit-led, recognizing God’s calling and gifting (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="20" data-verses="28" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 20:28</span>).

- **Tested and Approved** - Candidates had to demonstrate maturity and faithfulness before being entrusted with leadership (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Timothy" data-chapter="3" data-verses="10" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Timothy 3:10</span>). They cannot be recent converts! ((<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Timothy" data-chapter="3" data-verses="6" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Timothy 3:6</span>)

### Deacons (Servants)

Role and Responsibilities:

- **Practical Service** - Focused on meeting physical needs in the church, freeing elders to focus on prayer and teaching (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="6" data-verses="1-4" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 6:1-4</span>).

- **Support Ministry** - Assist with hospitality, caring for the poor, and administrative tasks (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="6" data-verses="3" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 6:3</span>).

- **Promote Unity** - Address issues of fairness and care to prevent division (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="6" data-verses="1-7" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 6:1-7</span>).

- **Examples of Faith** - Serve as role models in humility, faith, and service (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Timothy" data-chapter="3" data-verses="13" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Timothy 3:13</span>).

Selection Process (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="6" data-verses="1-6" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 6:1-6</span>):

- **Recognized by the Congregation** - Chosen based on their reputation and spiritual character.

- **Approved by Leaders** - Apostles reviewed and commissioned the chosen deacons through prayer and laying on of hands.

- **Confirmed in Service** - Deacons were first tested to prove their faithfulness before being officially appointed (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Timothy" data-chapter="3" data-verses="10" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Timothy 3:10</span>).

### Qualifications for Leadership

#### Qualifications for Both Elders and Deacons:

- Moral Character - Both must be above reproach, dignified, and demonstrate self-control (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Timothy" data-chapter="3" data-verses="2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Timothy 3:2</span>, 8).

- Family Life - Both must be faithful in marriage (“husband of one wife”) and manage their households well (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Timothy" data-chapter="3" data-verses="4-5" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Timothy 3:4-5</span>, 12).

- Spiritual Maturity - Both must be tested and proven before appointment (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Timothy" data-chapter="3" data-verses="6" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Timothy 3:6</span>, 10).

- Reputation - Both need a good reputation inside and outside the church (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Timothy" data-chapter="3" data-verses="7" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Timothy 3:7</span>).

- Faith and Integrity - Both must hold to the faith with a clear conscience and live out their beliefs (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Timothy" data-chapter="3" data-verses="9" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Timothy 3:9</span>).

### Example Structures

Using what we’ve learned so far about rules and structures for leadership, let’s look at two examples for church structure found in the New Testament, Jerusalem and Antioch.

### Church at Jerusalem

The church at Jerusalem was led primarily by the **apostles**, who focused on teaching, prayer, and spiritual oversight (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="2" data-verses="42" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 2:42</span>, <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="6" data-verses="4" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 6:4</span>). As the community grew, the need for administrative structure became apparent, particularly to address practical matters like caring for widows and distributing resources. This led to the appointment of **deacons** (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="6" data-verses="1-7" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 6:1-7</span>). These individuals, chosen for their spiritual maturity and good reputation, handled practical ministries to allow the apostles to focus on their spiritual responsibilities. Over time, **elders** also emerged as leaders in the Jerusalem church. For instance, during the Jerusalem Council (Acts 15), both apostles and elders were involved in decision-making, showing a collaborative leadership structure.

### Church at Antioch

The church at Antioch had a more decentralized and diverse leadership structure compared to Jerusalem. It was led by a group of **prophets and teachers**, including Barnabas, Saul (Paul), Simeon, Lucius, and Manaen (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="13" data-verses="1" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 13:1</span>). The church at Antioch was also much more oriented towards reaching the gentiles, where the church in Jerusalem reflected more of its Jewish roots. While the New Testament does not explicitly mention deacons in Antioch, it is likely that practical roles existed given the church's growing size and involvement in sending relief to Jerusalem (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="11" data-verses="29-30" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 11:29-30</span>). The church at Antioch became a model for commissioning missionaries, with leaders praying, fasting, and laying hands on Paul and Barnabas to send them out (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="13" data-verses="2-3" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 13:2-3</span>). This demonstrates a structure that balanced spiritual oversight (prophets and teachers) with missional outreach and practical support.

Unlike Paul’s corrections to the Corinthians and Galatians, the scriptures do not critique the leadership structures of Jerusalem or Antioch. This suggests that these churches, under the guidance of the Holy Spirit, were free to adapt their gatherings to meet their unique needs and circumstances within the framework of sound doctrine and apostolic teaching (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="2" data-verses="42" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 2:42</span>, <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="2 Timothy" data-chapter="3" data-verses="16-17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">2 Timothy 3:16-17</span>).

Throughout these passages certain terms are used that we have not yet defined and studied: *apostle, prophet, evangelist*. These gifts are part of the fivefold ministry that Jesus gave to the church.

### The Fivefold Ministry Gifts

Jesus established five distinct ministry gifts, often referred to as the **fivefold ministry gifts**, to equip believers for service and build up the Body of Christ.


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="4" data-verses="11-13" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Ephesians 4:11-13</u>** (NKJV) **11** *And He Himself gave some to be apostles, some prophets, some evangelists, and some pastors and teachers,* ***12** for the equipping of the saints for the work of ministry, for the edifying of the body of Christ,* ***13** till we all come to the unity of the faith and of the knowledge of the Son of God, to a perfect man, to the measure of the stature of the fullness of Christ;*

</span>


The five gifts given to equip the saints for the work of the ministry are:

1.  **Apostles** - Pioneers and visionaries who establish foundations, plant churches, and advance the Kingdom of God (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="2" data-verses="20" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 2:20</span>). Apostles model boldness and faith, demonstrating how to step out into new territories and take spiritual initiative.

2.  **Prophets** - Messengers and guides who proclaim God’s truth, bringing encouragement, edification, and direction (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="14" data-verses="3" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 14:3</span>). Prophets inspire faith, call others to align with God’s Word, and demonstrate reliance on hearing God’s voice.

3.  **Evangelists** - Proclaimers of the Gospel who focus on reaching the lost and stirring others to action in sharing Christ (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="8" data-verses="5-8" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 8:5-8</span>). Evangelists teach disciples how to share their faith effectively, model boldness in witnessing, and inspire compassion for the lost.

4.  **Pastors (Shepherds)** - Caretakers and nurturers who protect, guide, and provide spiritual oversight for the church (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="20" data-verses="28" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 20:28</span>). Pastors teach disciples the importance of community, care for others, and accountability within the body of Christ.

5.  **Teachers** - Instructors and trainers who clarify doctrine and help believers grow in understanding and application of God’s Word (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="2 Timothy" data-chapter="2" data-verses="15" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">2 Timothy 2:15</span>). Teachers equip disciples to study Scripture deeply, apply it in their daily lives, and effectively teach others.

## Leadership Versus Gifting

A commonly misunderstood aspect of leadership in the church is the separation between the authority and responsibility of leadership, and specific giftings.

This confusion is centered on our use of words like teacher, paster, prophet, apostle, evangelist. These words are gifts. There is not an **office** of ‘apostle’ in the New Testament church. There are people who do the work of an apostle, who have the gift or calling of apostle. Having the gift of apostle does not give them authority of responsibility.

I am a skilled musician; I have played saxophone for over 20 years. I played through school and college and played semi-professionally for several summers while working with an evangelistic team. Despite me having the skill, gift, and ability to be a saxophonist, I do not have the right to come into your church and stand on the stage with the worship team.

Likewise, there are people gifted in being a pastor/shepherd, but that does not make them ‘THE PASTOR’ of the local church or make them ‘your’ pastor. The five-fold ministry gifts do not lead the church, the leaders (elders and deacons) do. It is very common for leaders to have specific five-fold ministry gifts, but that gift enhances their leadership, it doesn’t create it. Likewise, it's common for still-developing people with fivefold giftings to serve and aspire to leadership, but God is first interested in character, then interested in giftings. We are all to become mature, able to discern the word of God, full of the spirit, and doing good works.

Every leader and every gift must first submit to being a disciple, being part of the body.

Jesus gave specific ministry gifts for the equipping of the saints for the work of the ministry. These are different from the motivational gifts (discussed later in this chapter) or manifestation gifts (discussed in the chapter on the baptisms). As disciples grow, the five-fold ministry gifts equip them for ministry, while elders and deacons provide oversight to ensure sound doctrine and practice.

## Your Role in the Church

So far in this chapter we’ve covered the leadership of the church and the core analogies for the church. Let’s build upon the specific analogy of *the body* and consider a marathon runner.

### You Are a Part of the Whole

The church, as the Body of Christ, depends on every member working together to fulfill its mission. Just as the body cannot function properly if one part is missing, the church needs each believer to actively participate, grow, and serve. Each member of the body has a very important role. To be part of the body means staying connected. A body can survive at diminished capacity without a limb, but the limb itself cannot live when separated from the body. We must be an active part of the church and not become dismembered. <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="10" data-verses="24" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 10:24</span> says that we should gather and not forsake the assembly.

To really work and function together you need to fellowship! You need to know others, love others and ideally like others! Build strong relationships and friendships and repair them when they become stressed or injured. When a joint gets injured or inflamed, you need to work to rest and or reset it; so that the joint that’s dislodged doesn’t become broken! A broken and unhealing limb will eventually be separated, and you cannot be part of the body, alone.

We don’t all have the same function. Some of us may share the Gospel from a stage, others around a dinner table. But we are ALL called to be ministers of reconciliation (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="2 Corinthians" data-chapter="5" data-verses="18-19" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">2 Corinthians 5:18-19</span>). We have all been received into the priesthood of believers (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Peter" data-chapter="2" data-verses="9" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Peter 2:9</span>, <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Revelation" data-chapter="1" data-verses="6" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Revelation 1:6</span>). This means that we're all supposed to be able to do the work of the ministry by the power of the Holy Spirit.

The toes work with the feet, the feet with the ankles, and so on-each part contributing to the overall mission. Marathon runners must train if they want to be able to complete the race. You need to be ready-not just by learning, but by actually practicing what you’ve learned. The church, like a marathon runner, thrives when each member plays their role in unison.

There are a great number of people in your life that only you can possibly reach. The Pastor can't be the only one trying to shepherd them, and the Evangelist can't be the only one trying to reach them. God wants to use you to reach unbelievers by the power of His Holy Spirit. We all do the work of the ministry; the Holy Spirit helps us.

### You Need Training

Believers must train and equip for their unique roles in the church, just as a runner strengthens specific muscles for the race. You won’t be successful if you jump into a marathon and haven’t trained, and not all training is equal, you need to be adequately prepared for the race. Jesus has given specific ministry gifts to equip believers, not to do all the work themselves, but to empower every member to serve effectively in their unique roles.

Training for a marathon will bring about transformation. You need strength and you need to transform. It’s our responsibility to submit ourselves to God and let him transform us in renewal (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="12" data-verses="1-2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 12:1-2</span>). Transformation should lead you to maturity, enabling you to bear your own loads and support others. You should be able to bear MUCH fruit. This will be discussed at length in the chapter on sanctification.

Training is hard, sometimes it’s uncomfortable. Sometimes the transformation you need to go through you’d much rather skip. But if you want to accomplish the goal, you need to stay on the narrow path. At times, you might need warning, comfort, or support-all offered with patience (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Thessalonians" data-chapter="5" data-verses="14" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Thessalonians 5:14</span>).

Every great marathon runner has a coach to train them, you will need a trainer to help you. Of course, you have the Holy Spirit, but He often relies on His coaches, the mature men and women of God, in the Body of Christ that you are called to work alongside.

As individuals we need to submit ourselves first and foremost to God and secondly, in this context, to a body of believers. Submission in the church means honoring godly leadership (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="13" data-verses="17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 13:17</span>) and submitting to one another in humility (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="5" data-verses="21" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 5:21</span>).

Submission can be difficult, especially when you have experienced broken trust or failed leadership previously. This does not imply blind obedience or control but reflects a spirit of reverence and cooperation. Worldly submission is demanded by those in authority and can be degrading and lead to resentment. Biblical submission rooted in humility and discernment strengthens the church and fosters accountability. Be willing to listen to guidance and constructive feedback.

Now that we’ve set a strong foundation - you a meaningful part of the body but need training - let’s get down to finding where you fit.

### Finding Where You Fit

Finding your role in the church involves seeking God’s guidance, understanding your spiritual gifts, and actively engaging with your church community. It is a journey of prayer, exploration, and growth, where you discover how God has uniquely equipped you to contribute to His kingdom. It's very important to note that your role in the church will likely change over time and is not likely to be the same throughout your entire life.

Here’s a practical approach to help you find your place, they are presented as a series of steps for clarity, but you will likely repeat the same step multiple times over the course of your walk with God.

#### Step 1 - Seek God’s Guidance Through Prayer and the Word

Start by **seeking God’s guidance through prayer**. God is the ultimate source of your purpose and calling (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Jeremiah" data-chapter="29" data-verses="11" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Jeremiah 29:11</span>). Pray regularly for clarity about how you can serve in the church, asking Him to reveal your spiritual gifts and passions. Trust that He will direct your steps as you surrender your plans to His will (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Proverbs" data-chapter="3" data-verses="5-6" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Proverbs 3:5-6</span>).

**Study the Word of God** to deepen your understanding of the church’s mission and your role within it. Key passages such as 1 Corinthians 12 and <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="4" data-verses="11-12" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 4:11-12</span> highlight spiritual gifts and the diversity of roles in the Body of Christ. Reflect on examples of individuals in Scripture who discovered and fulfilled their roles, like Paul, Timothy, and Lydia.

#### Step 2 - Identify Your Gifts, Passions, and Skills

Identifying your **spiritual gifts** is an essential step. You may find taking a spiritual gifts assessment helpful. These gifts are given by God to build up the church. There are several classes of gifts listed in the New Testament:

- **Motivational Gifts** given by God: <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="12" data-verses="6-8" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 12:6-8</span>

  - Discussed after this list.

- Five-Fold Ministry Gifts given by Jesus: <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="4" data-verses="11-13" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 4:11-13</span>

  - Discussed previously in this chapter.

- Manifestation Gifts given by the Holy Spirit: <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="12" data-verses="8-10" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 12:8-10</span>

  - Discussed in the chapter on baptism.

The **motivational gifts** reflect how a believer is wired to think, feel, and act. They motivate believers to serve in specific ways that build up the church. Each gift complements the others, ensuring the church operates as a unified body with diverse roles (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="12" data-verses="4-5" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 12:4-5</span>). They operate as grace-given abilities (Greek: ***charis** - khar'-ece - **G5463 -** an unearned gift*) meaning that they are given from God, not earned or learned. While the gifts come from God, a person who has a gift can become more proficient in using it.

The motivational gifts are:

1.  **Prophecy** - A God-given inclination to boldly declare truth, often with a focus on conviction and righteousness, guiding others to align with God’s Word.\*

2.  **Service (Helps)** - Meets practical needs through acts of service and assistance to support others.

3.  **Teaching** - Explains and clarifies truth, helping others understand and apply God’s Word.

4.  **Exhortation (Encouragement)** - Motivates, encourages, and comforts others to inspire growth and perseverance.

5.  **Giving** - Provides resources generously to meet needs and advance God’s work without seeking recognition.

6.  **Leadership (Administration)** - Organizes and directs others to accomplish goals with vision and efficiency.

7.  **Mercy** - Shows compassion and care to comfort and support those who are hurting or in need.

\* Prophecy used in the context of Romans is understood to be different than prophecy as a Holy Spirit manifestation gift (1 Corinthians). The Greek word used is the same in both scriptures, but the context is different. For a more in-depth study of the difference, I recommend reading:

- *The Gift of Prophecy* by Kenneth E. Hagin ([www.rhema.org](http://www.rhema.org))

<!-- -->

- *The Gifts and Ministries of the Holy Spirit* by Lester Sumrall

As you identify your gifts, consider your **passions and skills**. God often calls us to serve in areas we’re passionate about or skilled in (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Exodus" data-chapter="31" data-verses="2-6" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Exodus 31:2-6</span>). Reflect on what excites you about serving God and others, and think about how your professional skills, hobbies, and life experiences might contribute to ministry.

Seek guidance from **leaders and mentors** who can provide wisdom and insight (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Proverbs" data-chapter="15" data-verses="22" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Proverbs 15:22</span>). Meet with a pastor or ministry leader to discuss your interests and ask for honest feedback about where you might fit best. Pray about the feedback you receive. Consider joining a discipleship group to receive training and ongoing support.

#### Step 3 - Be Faithful Now

The parable of the talents (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="25" data-verses="14-30" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 25:14-30</span>) reminds us that we need to be faithful in little things to be trusted in great things (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Luke" data-chapter="16" data-verses="10" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Luke 16:10</span>).

**Get involved and serve** in various ministries to explore where God is calling you. Start small by volunteering in areas of need within your church and in ministries they support. For example:

- Join the hospitality team, children’s ministry, or outreach programs. Consider leading a discipleship-oriented group for new believers on this book *Principles of Faith,* using the group discussions questions and end-of-chapter activities to facilitate dialogue.

- Be open to stepping outside your comfort zone to discover new opportunities.

- Observe where God works through you and where you feel most effective.

Practice giving. We can give to the church in many important ways, and we are instructed to do so to accomplish the mission.

- Time - we can give our time and volunteer. When we give our time, we use our efforts and our talents. This can also be a great way to learn new skills and increase existing ones.

- Resources - we can give of our resources. Most commonly today this is probably giving financially to the church and to ministries (not just the “institution”, but those in need in the church). Giving money is useful, because the church can buy needed resources with it, but people have given their physical resources beyond money, for example extra homes, clothing, cars, food, building materials, tools, etc. In <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="4" data-verses="32-35" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 4:32-35</span> we see an example of early believers giving freely, sharing proceeds from sale of extra lands and homes and giving so that ‘none had need’.

Working alongside others in ministry can reveal your strengths and deepen your understanding of your role. Engage in small groups or church activities to foster connections. Listen to the burdens and needs of those around you and consider how you can support them. Building relationships within the church helps you connect with others and understand the community’s needs.

#### Step 4 - Get Help!

Jesus said that in life we would experience trouble! (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="16" data-verses="33" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 16:33</span>) We are encouraged to not carry the weight of our burdens by ourselves but to cast them on God who cares for us! (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Peter" data-chapter="5" data-verses="7" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Peter 5:7</span>)

Sometimes in our journey we may become injured. Marathon runners, even when doing “everything right” can stress their bodies to the point of injury, or they can trip and fall.

Not all ‘falling’ is sin related, but some of it is, not all weakness is ‘our fault’, sometimes others will sin against us. Regardless for the cause of our weakness or injury we are to be restored and healed.

There is great freedom in seeking inner healing and restoration. We are called to cover the vulnerable parts of the body (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="12" data-verses="23" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 12:23</span>) and to restore those caught in trespasses (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Galatians" data-chapter="6" data-verses="1" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Galatians 6:1</span>).

There is much to say about this topic, and we explore the themes of healing as an overview in the chapter on sanctification.

#### Step 5 - Be Patient

Pay attention to confirmation from God and others. Often, God confirms your calling through fruitfulness, joy, and affirmation from those around you. Reflect on where your efforts have borne fruit and listen to feedback about the impact of the ministry. This can help you recognize where you are most effective in serving the Body of Christ.

Be patient and trust God’s timing. Discovering your role may take time and involve trial and error (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Philippians" data-chapter="1" data-verses="6" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Philippians 1:6</span>). Trust that God will guide you as you remain faithful and open to His leading. During this time, commit to growth and training by attending workshops, Bible studies, and other resources that equip you for service. Do this with the church, engage with the body of Christ to discover your gifting.

Remember, the church is not a building or an institution; it is a living, breathing body made up of people like you. By stepping into your role, you contribute to its mission and glorify Christ as its head.

## Personal Reflection Questions 

1.  What does it mean to you personally to be a “called-out one”? How can you live out this identity in your daily life?

2.  Which of the biblical images of the church (Body, Bride, Temple, etc.) resonates most with you? Why?

3.  Reflect on your role in the Body of Christ. What gifts, talents, or passions has God given you to serve His church?

4.  How does being part of a local church strengthen your faith and help you fulfill God’s mission?

5.  What steps can you take to foster unity, accountability, and growth in your church community?

## Group Discussion Questions (20-30 Minutes)

1.  **Icebreaker Question:** “What is your earliest memory of attending or being part of a church? How has your view of the church changed over time?”

2.  **Exploring the Church as the Body of Christ:** Read <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="12" data-verses="12-27" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 12:12-27</span>. What does this passage teach us about the church’s unity and diversity? How can we celebrate and honor each other’s differences?

3.  **The Church’s Mission:** How does the church fulfill its upward (worship), inward (edification), and outward (evangelism) purposes? Which area do you feel most called to participate in?

4.  **Addressing Conflict and Growth:** <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="12" data-verses="12-17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 12:12-17</span> calls us to strengthen one another and avoid bitterness. How can we handle division or hurt within the church in ways that foster healing and unity?

5.  **Finding Your Place:** Think about a time when you felt most connected to your church. What factors contributed to that experience, and how can we create similar experiences for others?

# Chapter 10 - Sanctified-Prepared for God’s Purpose

In the previous two chapters we have discussed several important topics. We explored the role and ministry of the Holy Spirit, as well as the role and ministry of the church.

We have a part to play in the church, and while we know that we need training, we also need purification. To really be prepared for God’s purposes we must be sanctified.

The definition of **sanctification** is to purify something for a special purpose. This is closely related to the term **consecration** which means to set something aside for a special purpose.

Imagine a master sculptor preparing to work with a large block of marble. The master imagines what this raw and unshaped marble can be transformed into through his careful craftsmanship. He has a very specific design in mind, he orders his assistants to set this block of marble aside for a special design (**consecration**). As he refines and works the marble, adding texture, depth, and shape, he purifies it (**sanctifies**) for that special design. The act of dedicating the marble for his special project was quick, whereas the process of shaping the marble into its final design takes considerable patience.

We were set aside, consecrated to God at salvation. Through the act of purifying our souls by the help of the Spirit we become transformed. This process of purification is what sanctifies us.

When we received Jesus through salvation, we were born-again. We were justified, our sins forgiven. Our spirit was regenerated, we became alive in Christ. We were adopted into the family of God and became the literal temple of the Holy Spirit. Such a marvelous transformation that the scriptures say, “*there is joy before the angels of God over one sinner who repents.” (*<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Luke" data-chapter="15" data-verses="10" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Luke 15:10</span>).

Receiving Jesus by salvation (**justification**) saves our spirit, cleanses us, and makes us presentable to God, able to receive His Holy Spirit. However, despite such a great salvation **our souls** (our mind, will, and emotions) still **need work**!

Was the work of salvation incomplete? No! The work of salvation is effective but must be continued in the life of the believer by the renewal brought on by the Holy Spirit. This process of renewal and transformation is the work of **sanctification.**

We know there is continued work to be done after conversion because our painful memories didn’t resolve, many of our beliefs stayed the same, and many of our habits or rituals didn’t vanish. Furthermore, conversion did not separate our minds from their fleshly (carnal) nature.

The word ‘carnal’ comes from the Latin root meaning ‘flesh’ or ‘meat.’ The carnal nature is inside of each of us from birth, like an animal’s instincts, it exists to help us survive. The way the carnal nature works is by being self-focused, self-oriented, self-centered. Children are natural examples of a fleshly nature. Despite their innocence, their nature is focused primarily on their wants, desires, needs; and ‘watch out!’ if you don’t meet its every expectation.

Part of growing up is maturing past our childish ways, but when pressed we very easily resort to a pattern of thinking and living oriented around the self. As James says:


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="James" data-chapter="4" data-verses="1" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>James 4:1</u>** (NKJV) *Where do wars and fights come from among you? Do they not come from your desires for pleasure that war in your members?*

</span>


Paul explains this carnal nature in more detail in his epistle to the Romans (chapters 6 - 8). He says this carnal nature keeps him doing what he hates and causes him to do evil. He describes this internal conflict as a war between his man-made nature and his God-made spirit.

This conflict between flesh and spirit exists in each of us, and it’s our job to do something about it. If we do not confront our carnal nature, our souls will remain entangled in patterns of spiritual decay, even though our spirits have been redeemed.


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="8" data-verses="6-8" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Romans 8:6-8</u>** (NKJV) **6** *For to be carnally minded is death, but to be spiritually minded is life and peace.* ***7** Because the carnal mind is enmity against God; for it is not subject to the law of God, nor indeed can be.* ***8** So then, those who are in the flesh cannot please God.*

</span>


These words of Paul are both a warning and a promise to us. Even though we’ve been born again, we still have a fleshly mind that needs to be brought into submission to the knowledge of Christ. As we do so, we walk in God’s life and peace.

Take courage, there is hope for us! We must walk after the Spirit and be led by the Spirit of God. In doing so, we will not walk after the flesh.


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="fragment" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="8" data-verses="1,13,14" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Romans 8:1,13,14</u>** (NKJV)
>
>**1** *There is therefore now no condemnation to those who are in Christ Jesus, who do not walk according to the flesh, but according to the Spirit…*
>
>***13** For if you live according to the flesh you will die; but if by the Spirit you put to death the deeds of the body, you will live.*
>
>***14** For as many as are led by the Spirit of God, these are sons of God.*

</span>


How do we learn to walk in the Spirit? Is it *just a conscious choice*? We learn to walk in the Spirit the same way we learn to walk in the natural. We are motivated to move and grow, so we wrestle, struggle, crawl, totter, till we can stand on our own feet. Our natural parents can’t walk for us, but they can support, encourage, help, and enable us in our walk.

Likewise, God cannot walk out our faith for us. He will teach us, but we must put in the effort. As Paul said to the Philippians:  
  

<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Philippians" data-chapter="2" data-verses="12-13" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Philippians 2:12-13</u>** (NKJV) **12** *Therefore, my beloved, as you have always obeyed, not as in my presence only, but now much more in my absence, work out your own salvation with fear and trembling; **13** for it is God who works in you both to will and to do for His good pleasure.*

</span>


We must work out our own salvation. That doesn't mean that we climb atop a wooden cross and are physically crucified, but we do have to crucify our fleshly nature (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Galatians" data-chapter="5" data-verses="24" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Galatians 5:24</span>). God promises that He will work in us and help us, but we must make a choice to walk this out, and we must stick to it.

It’s true to say that ‘we’ are dealing with our fleshly nature, but it’s also true to say that ‘God’ is dealing with it. The work of the cross (justification) was difficult for Jesus, He had to sacrifice His life for ours, but easy for us, it's a free gift. The work of the spirit (sanctification) is easy for God, He walks in perfect truth and is willing and desirous to abide with us, but is difficult for us, because we must crucify our old nature, and our nature isn’t very interested in being crucified.

This process is collaborative. God helps, aids, guides, directs, and corrects. We listen, obey, follow, and respond. The result is growth and transformation. This is the pruning that Jesus said God would do in us. Jesus is the vine, His father the vinedresser, we are the branches:


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="John" data-chapter="15" data-verses="1-4" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>John 15:1-4</u>** (NKJV)
>
>***1** "I am the true vine, and My Father is the vinedresser.*
>
>***2** Every branch in Me that does not bear fruit He takes away; and every branch that bears fruit He prunes, that it may bear more fruit.*
>
>***3** You are already clean because of the word which I have spoken to you.*
>
>***4** Abide in Me, and I in you. As the branch cannot bear fruit of itself, unless it abides in the vine, neither can you, unless you abide in Me.*

</span>


Pruning is incredibly healthy for a plant. Thinking back to our chapter on repentance, we are called to turn away from dead works (those works that are no longer bearing fruit) and turn toward God. The result of this turning away from the dead places in our life, and towards God, empowers the great gardener of our life to prune branches, improve growth, and produce greater spiritual fruit (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="15" data-verses="2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 15:2</span>).

While pruning can feel painful, it reflects God’s loving care as He shapes us into vessels for His purpose (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="12" data-verses="11" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 12:11</span>). Some may feel a sense of loss, even fear, when undergoing the pruning process. However, God is gentle, and even a bruised reed He will not break (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Isaiah" data-chapter="42" data-verses="3" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Isaiah 42:3</span>). Grapevines are pruned during their dormant season to minimize stress on the plant. The gardener tending the growth will cut back old and woody growth that no longer produces fruit like it used to, this allows the vine to direct energy into the branches with the most potential for good fruit.

Like a gardener trimming branches to direct growth, God may allow seasons of correction and discipline to refine our faith and character, but His work is patient and kind. God prunes those who abide in him. Our focus is to be more on the abiding in Christ, and less on the work of the pruning.

<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="12" data-verses="11" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 12:11</span> reminds us that pruning “*yields the peaceful fruit of righteousness*” to those who are trained by it.

How are we trained by righteousness? By being transformed through the renewal of our minds.


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="12" data-verses="1-2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Romans 12:1-2</u>** (NKJV) ***1** I beseech you therefore, brethren, by the mercies of God, that you present your bodies a living sacrifice, holy, acceptable to God, which is your reasonable service. **2** And do not be conformed to this world, but be <u>transformed</u> by the <u>renewing</u> of your mind, that you may prove what is that good and acceptable and perfect will of God.* (emphasis added)

</span>


Paul lays out two simple requirements for transformation:

<u>1. Present your bodies to God as a living sacrifice</u>. A **living** sacrifice doesn't physically die during the sacrifice, but since a living sacrifice cannot die, you must continually bring it to the altar!

<u>2. Renew your mind!</u> Renewal, based on the Greek word: **Anakainōsis** (*An-ak-ah'-ee-no-sis -* **G342**) means a renewal or renovation, focusing on a continual process of inner change and growth.

This renewal brings about transformation. Transformation is based on the Greek word: **Metamorphoō** (*met-am-or-fo'-o -* **G3339**) means to change into another form; to transform or transfigure, emphasizing a complete change. Metamorphoō is where we get the English word metamorphosis, which is the process caterpillars undergo to become butterflies.

God wants to fundamentally transform our minds and our pattern of thinking. His desire is for us not to be conformed to the way of the world, but that we will know what is good (beneficial), pleasing (acceptable), and the perfect (complete & lacking nothing) will of God.

The requirements for transformation are really that simple, **though it takes discipline**!

Our fight against the flesh will not be won by our flesh, but rather by God’s equipping for the battle of the mind. His words (the sword of truth) are mighty to correct every imagination, haughty thought, and to bring into arrest (captivity) every thought that goes against obeying God.


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="2 Corinthians" data-chapter="10" data-verses="3-6" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>2 Corinthians 10:3-6</u>** (NKJV)
>
> ***3** For though we walk in the flesh, we do not war to the flesh.*
>
> ***4** For the weapons of our warfare are not carnal but mighty in God for pulling down strongholds,*
>
> ***5** casting down arguments and every high thing that exalts itself against the knowledge of God, bringing every thought into captivity to the obedience of Christ,*
>
> ***6** and being ready to punish all disobedience when your obedience is fulfilled.*

</span>


When the authorities arrest some, they place them in shackles and put them in a jail cell. If that person escapes custody, they don't turn a blind eye, they will use incredible resources and not rest until that person has been brought back under control. We too should act with such zeal in the contest for our minds!

We are renewed by obedience and experience with truth. Jesus said if we continued in His words, we would be His disciples, experience (<u>know</u>) truth, and that would make us free (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="8" data-verses="31-32" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 8:31-32</span>)!

The experience of truth is different than the knowledge of truth. Experience is the Greek word **Ginōskō** (Ghin-oce'-ko **- G1097**) it means to know through personal experience or intimate understanding; implies progressive learning.

It is not our academic knowledge of truth that transforms us and renews our minds, it is our personal experience of truth. We are sanctified by His truth! (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="17" data-verses="17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 17:17</span>).

## How to Renew Your Mind

Renewing our minds is not a one-size-fits-all process-it depends on which part of the mind we’re talking about! God designed our minds with incredible complexity, including several different memory systems, each serving a specific purpose. Some operate automatically in the background, while others require focused, intentional thought. These systems are gifts from God to help us survive, grow, and thrive, but they can also lead us astray when misused or left unrenewed. Understanding how these systems work gives us insight into how to cooperate with God’s process of transformation.

In the next section I will describe each of the memory systems, their purpose and function, some practical steps to renewing this aspect of your mind, as well as resources for continued practice and study.

### **Memory Systems and How to Renew Them** 

#### **1. Explicit Memory (Declarative Memory)**

**Purpose:** Stores facts, events, and experiences that we can consciously recall. It’s split into two types-**semantic** and **episodic** memory.

**Semantic Memory - Facts and Knowledge.** Semantic memory is where we store facts, definitions, and general knowledge-things like historical dates, Bible verses, and practical information. It helps us predict outcomes, make decisions, and avoid danger (e.g., recognizing poisonous plants). While it gives us stability and order, it can also lead to rigidity-an over-reliance on rules rather than adapting to unexpected situations.

**How to Renew It:**

- **Study Scripture** to shape our worldview (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Psalm" data-chapter="119" data-verses="105" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Psalm 119:105</span>).

- **Memorize truths** and doctrines to stand firm in faith (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Colossians" data-chapter="3" data-verses="16" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Colossians 3:16</span>).

- **Read Christian books** like *"Renewing the Mind" by Casey Treat* or *"Battlefield of the Mind" by Joyce Meyer* to develop godly thinking patterns.

**Episodic Memory - Personal Experiences.** Episodic memory stores our personal experiences, tying them to specific times and places-like a wedding day or a traumatic event. These memories provide context, helping us learn from the past and build relationships. However, episodic memory is prone to emotional bias and distortion. Past trauma can create fearful associations, making it difficult to move forward.

**How to Renew It:**

- **Reflect and confess** to bring hidden pain into God’s light (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 John" data-chapter="1" data-verses="9" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 John 1:9</span>).

- **Reframe past experiences** through the lens of God’s grace (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="8" data-verses="28" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 8:28</span>).

- **Seek inner healing** through prayer and counseling (*“Your Pain is Showing” by Larry F. Low, "The Bondage Breaker" by Neil T. Anderson*, *"Emotionally Healthy Spirituality" by Peter Scazzero*).

#### **2. Implicit Memory (Non-Declarative Memory)**

**Purpose:** Unconscious memory used for habits, skills, and emotional associations. It operates automatically, helping us respond quickly to situations. It’s divided into **procedural memory** and **emotional conditioning**.

**Procedural Memory - Skills and Habits.** Procedural memory allows us to perform tasks without thinking-like driving, typing, or playing an instrument. It’s efficient, saving us mental energy and time. However, bad habits and negative patterns can also become deeply ingrained and hard to break.

**How to Renew It:**

- **Practice spiritual disciplines** like prayer and fasting to build godly habits (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="5" data-verses="14" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 5:14</span>).

- **Train in godliness** through repetition and obedience (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Timothy" data-chapter="4" data-verses="7" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Timothy 4:7</span>).

- **Read books like** *"Atomic Habits" by James Clear* (practical) and *"Spiritual Disciplines for the Christian Life" by Donald Whitney* (biblical) to establish habits that honor God.

**Emotional Conditioning - Emotional Associations.** Emotional conditioning links sensory cues (like smells or sounds) to feelings, triggering responses before we consciously process them. This system protects us but can also cause irrational fears or emotional reactions when past experiences are negative.

**How to Renew It:**

- **Pray for healing** from past wounds (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Isaiah" data-chapter="41" data-verses="10" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Isaiah 41:10</span>).

- **Practice forgiveness** and release bitterness (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="4" data-verses="31-32" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 4:31-32</span>).

- **Replace fear with God’s promises** (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="2 Timothy" data-chapter="1" data-verses="7" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">2 Timothy 1:7</span>).

- **Seek guidance from books like** *“Your Pain is Showing” by Larry F. Low, "The Bondage Breaker" by Neil T. Anderson*, “*Soul Care" by Rob Reimer* for overcoming emotional triggers.

#### **3. Working Memory (Short-Term Memory)**

**Purpose:** Provides temporary storage for problem-solving and decision-making. It helps us focus, hold information briefly, and process tasks-like remembering instructions or making quick decisions. While it’s powerful, it’s also limited, prone to distraction, and easily overloaded, especially under stress.

**How to Renew It:**

- **Focus and meditate** on God’s Word daily (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Psalm" data-chapter="1" data-verses="2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Psalm 1:2</span>).

- **Renew your thoughts** through intentional thinking (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="12" data-verses="2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 12:2</span>).

- **Practice mindfulness** and simplify mental clutter (*"Habits of Grace" by David Mathis*).

- **Read** *"The Celebration of Discipline" by Richard Foster* to sharpen focus through spiritual disciplines.

## Conclusion

Renewing the mind isn’t a quick fix-it’s a process of gradual transformation that is lifelong. Each memory system plays a role in shaping how we think, feel, and act. By intentionally working with the Holy Spirit, applying Scripture, and developing godly habits, we can train our minds to align with God’s truth. Whether addressing past wounds, learning new habits, or staying focused in the moment, God has provided both **spiritual tools** and **practical wisdom** to help us walk in renewal and freedom (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="2 Corinthians" data-chapter="10" data-verses="5" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">2 Corinthians 10:5</span>).

Our world is full of trouble, tribulation, and trial. Through the disciplined renewing of the mind, our souls become transformed, and we can live a life of freedom unto God full of the fruit of His Spirit (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Galatians" data-chapter="5" data-verses="22-25" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Galatians 5:22-25</span>).

### Acknowledgements

The description of the memory systems contained in this chapter is borrowed from the study of psychology: [<u>https://www.psypost.org/implicit-vs-explicit-memory-definition-examples-history/</u>](https://www.psypost.org/implicit-vs-explicit-memory-definition-examples-history/). The how-to-renew section comes from a combination of sources: the books referenced in this section, several of which I've read, and the rest come highly recommended by practitioners in the field, my own life experience of renewing my mind, and several years of experience as a pastor helping people personally grow through painful spots in their lives. Special thanks to Larry Low ([<u>www.larrylow.com</u>](http://www.larrylow.com)) for all his help in teaching me to renew my mind and help others how to do so!

## Personal Reflection Questions 

1.  What does sanctification mean to you personally? How do you see it playing out in your daily life?

2.  Think about a time when God “pruned” an area of your life. What was the result of this process, and how did it draw you closer to Him?

3.  Reflect on the idea of “renewing your mind.” What specific thought patterns or habits might God be calling you to surrender or transform?

4.  How does seeing sanctification as a partnership with the Holy Spirit encourage you during difficult or slow seasons of growth?

5.  How can you intentionally make space for the Holy Spirit to work in your daily life? What might you need to let go of to do this?

6.  Where do you need renewal today? Start by identifying the areas where your mind feels stuck and trust that God will finish the good work He began in you (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Philippians" data-chapter="1" data-verses="6" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Philippians 1:6</span>).

## Group Discussion Questions (20-30 Minutes)

1.  **Icebreaker Question:** “What’s one area of your life where you’ve experienced noticeable growth since coming to faith in Christ?”

2.  **Exploring Scripture:** Read <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="15" data-verses="1-5" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 15:1-5</span>. How does the image of pruning and bearing fruit help us understand sanctification?

3.  **Renewing the Mind:** Discuss <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="12" data-verses="2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 12:2</span>. What does it mean to “renew your mind,” and how does this relate to sanctification?

4.  **The Role of the Holy Spirit:** How does the Holy Spirit empower and guide us in the process of sanctification? How can we become more attuned to His leading?

5.  **Encouragement in the Journey:** What would you say to someone who feels discouraged by the pace of sanctification? How can we encourage one another in this journey?

# Chapter 11 - Baptisms-Water and Spirit

Baptism in our modern age, separated from the Jewish roots, is typically only thought of in terms of water baptism, and the Holy Spirit baptism (Pentecost).

The book of Hebrews was written to a Jewish audience. The overwhelming majority of Christians in our modern age do not have Jewish roots or heritage. Without the Jewish roots and culture many of us do not have the cultural context for things that 1st century messianic Jews (those Jewish believers professing Jesus) would have shared. As we were not raised in the Jewish tradition we don’t typically know much about the feasts, fasts, prayers, washings, rites, and rituals that Jewish believers would know.

The word for baptisms, or washings, in the Greek is: **Baptismos** (bap-tis-mos’ - **G909**) - The act of dipping or washing. Refers to ceremonial washings or ritual cleansing. It can indicate immersion, purification rites, or washings practiced in Jewish and Christian contexts.

Baptisms is one of the foundational doctrines mentioned in Hebrews:


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="6" data-verses="1-2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Hebrews 6:1-2</u>** (NKJV) ***1** Therefore, leaving the discussion of the elementary principles of Christ, let us go on to perfection, not laying again the foundation of repentance from dead works and of faith toward God, **2** of the doctrine of baptisms, of laying on of hands, of resurrection of the dead, and of eternal judgment.*

</span>


## Old Covenant Washings - Historical Context

In the Old Covenant, ceremonial washings played a central role in maintaining ritual purity and symbolizing holiness before God. These washings were prescribed in the Law of Moses and applied both to the priests who served in the Tabernacle and to the people of Israel. For example, priests were required to wash their hands and feet at the bronze basin before entering the Tabernacle or approaching the altar, symbolizing their readiness to serve in the presence of a holy God (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Exodus" data-chapter="30" data-verses="18-21" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Exodus 30:18-21</span>). Similarly, rituals for cleansing individuals from defilement, such as contact with a dead body or skin diseases, involved washing with water as a sign of restoration to purity (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Leviticus" data-chapter="14" data-verses="8" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Leviticus 14:8</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Numbers" data-chapter="19" data-verses="17-19" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Numbers 19:17-19</span>). These acts served as outward demonstrations of an inward desire for holiness, emphasizing the importance of being set apart for God.

The ceremonial washings in the Old Testament required regular repetition to maintain ritual purity. Unlike permanent solutions, these rituals only provided temporary external cleansing, leaving the deeper issue of sin unresolved. The writer of Hebrews highlights this limitation, explaining that these practices were “symbolic for the present time,” unable to clear the conscience of the worshiper (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="9" data-verses="9-10" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 9:9-10</span>). These washings acted as constant reminders of human imperfection and the need for something greater-an ultimate purification that could cleanse not just the body, but the heart and soul as well.

The ceremonial washings of the Old Covenant find their fulfillment in Christ, who provides complete and eternal cleansing through His sacrifice. Unlike the temporary rituals of the law, Christ’s blood purifies fully, cleansing not just the flesh but also the conscience (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="9" data-verses="13-14" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 9:13-14</span>). Through His death and resurrection, Jesus accomplished what the Old Testament rituals could only foreshadow-true reconciliation with God. <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="10" data-verses="22" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 10:22</span> calls believers to “draw near with a true heart in full assurance of faith, having our hearts sprinkled clean from an evil conscience and our bodies washed with pure water.” This language ties Christ’s work to the imagery of Old Testament washings while showing its far superior efficacy. His sacrifice does not merely cover sin but removes it completely (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 John" data-chapter="1" data-verses="7" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 John 1:7</span>). In this way, the ceremonial washings served as a shadow, pointing to the deeper cleansing that only Christ could provide, fulfilling humanity’s need for holiness through divine intervention.

To reiterate this point, Christians are under no obligation to perform any ceremonial washing. Washings did not provide salvation under the old covenant and do not provide salvation in the new covenant. Ceremonial washing will not increase our standing with God, nor gain us favor.
