Paul strongly condemns the Judaizers of Galatia (read Galatians) who tried to mix in requirements of the Jewish context as necessary for salvation (e.g., circumcision). Salvation is by faith in Jesus alone!

## John’s Baptism

In the New Testament canon there are several baptisms mentioned. We’ve already discussed the baptisms and washings of the Jewish tradition.

John the Baptist’s ministry centered on preparing the way for the coming of Jesus Christ. His baptism, described as a baptism of repentance for the forgiveness of sins (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Mark" data-chapter="1" data-verses="4" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Mark 1:4</span>), called people to confess their sins, turn their hearts back to God, and prepare for the arrival of the Messiah. It served as an outward sign of inward repentance, symbolizing a desire for spiritual cleansing and readiness for the kingdom of God. John’s baptism, however, was temporary and preparatory-it pointed forward to the greater work that Christ would accomplish. While John’s baptism focused on repentance, it did not provide complete forgiveness or spiritual regeneration, as it lacked the indwelling and empowerment of the Holy Spirit (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="3" data-verses="11-12" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 3:11-12</span>). Instead, John openly declared that one greater than He was coming, who would baptize not only with water but with the Holy Spirit and fire (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Luke" data-chapter="3" data-verses="16" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Luke 3:16</span>).

John’s baptism was fulfilled and surpassed through the death, burial, and resurrection of Jesus Christ. Christian baptism, instituted by Jesus and practiced by the apostles, signifies a deeper reality-union with Christ, death to sin, and resurrection to new life (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="6" data-verses="3-4" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 6:3-4</span>). Unlike John’s baptism, Christian baptism is directly connected to faith in Jesus’ atoning sacrifice and the indwelling of the Holy Spirit (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="2" data-verses="38" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 2:38</span>). The arrival of the Holy Spirit at Pentecost marked the new covenant and the beginning of Spirit-empowered living, which John’s baptism could not provide. Because of this, John’s baptism is no longer practiced, as it has been replaced by the baptism commanded by Christ (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="28" data-verses="19" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 28:19</span>), which incorporates repentance, faith, and the sealing of believers by the Holy Spirit (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="1" data-verses="13-14" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 1:13-14</span>).

## Christ’s Baptism

Christ’s baptism set a powerful example for His followers and established the foundation for the Christian practice of water baptism. Though Jesus was without sin, He submitted to baptism by John to “fulfill all righteousness” (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="3" data-verses="15" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 3:15</span>). In doing so, He identified with humanity’s need for repentance and pointed forward to His own death, burial, and resurrection. At His baptism, the Holy Spirit descended like a dove, and the Father declared, “This is my beloved Son, with whom I am well pleased” (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="3" data-verses="16-17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 3:16-17</span>). This moment marked the beginning of Jesus’ public ministry and revealed the unity of the Father, Son, and Spirit, foreshadowing the spiritual transformation believers would experience through faith in Christ.

Jesus later commanded His followers to be baptized, commissioning them to “go and make disciples of all nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father, and of the Son, and of the Holy Spirit” (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="28" data-verses="19" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 28:19</span>). Water baptism, as instituted by Christ, became an **act of obedience** and a **public declaration of faith** for believers. It represents their identification with Jesus’ death, burial, and resurrection (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="6" data-verses="3-4" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 6:3-4</span>). Going under the water symbolizes dying to sin, and coming up out of the water illustrates rising to new life in Christ (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Colossians" data-chapter="2" data-verses="12" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Colossians 2:12</span>). Yet, it is crucial to clarify that this outward act is not the source of salvation but a **visible sign** of the **inner work of grace** already accomplished by the blood of Jesus.

Water baptism is a **symbol** of the cleansing power of Christ’s blood and the renewal of the Holy Spirit (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="5" data-verses="26" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 5:26</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Titus" data-chapter="3" data-verses="5" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Titus 3:5</span>). It signifies repentance, faith, and a commitment to follow Jesus. It is an outward testimony of an inward transformation-a way for believers to publicly declare their allegiance to Christ and their participation in the body of Christ, the Church (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="2" data-verses="41" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 2:41</span>). While it is commanded by Jesus and modeled in the early Church, it does not **save** a person. Salvation is by grace through faith alone (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="2" data-verses="8-9" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 2:8-9</span>), and the true cleansing occurs spiritually through the blood of Christ (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Revelation" data-chapter="1" data-verses="5" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Revelation 1:5</span>). Water baptism is a response to that salvation, an act of obedience rather than a requirement for it.

In the New Testament, water baptism is consistently tied to **repentance and faith** (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="2" data-verses="38" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 2:38</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Mark" data-chapter="16" data-verses="16" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Mark 16:16</span>), both of which require a personal decision to follow Christ-something infants are not capable of making. Baptism symbolizes a believer’s deliberate identification with Christ’s death, burial, and resurrection (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="6" data-verses="3-4" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 6:3-4</span>), as well as their commitment to live in obedience to Him. Some denominations (e.g., Catholics, Lutherans) practice infant baptism as a covenantal sign akin to circumcision, however because infants cannot express personal faith or repentance, the practice of **infant baptism** does not align with the biblical model of **believer’s baptism**. Instead, many churches practice **dedication ceremonies** for infants, following the example of Hannah dedicating Samuel to the Lord (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Samuel" data-chapter="1" data-verses="27-28" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Samuel 1:27-28</span>) and Mary and Joseph presenting Jesus at the temple (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Luke" data-chapter="2" data-verses="22" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Luke 2:22</span>). Dedication is not a substitute for baptism but rather a commitment by parents to raise their child in the faith, teaching them God’s Word, and modeling a godly life until the child is old enough to make their own decision to follow Christ and be baptized.

Unlike the **ceremonial washings** of the Old Covenant, which required repeated rituals for purification, Christian baptism is a one-time event that reflects a completed work of grace. Old Testament washings symbolized external cleansing and foreshadowed the deeper spiritual cleansing that comes through Christ. In contrast, New Testament baptism symbolizes the inward renewal and rebirth accomplished by the Holy Spirit (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Titus" data-chapter="3" data-verses="5" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Titus 3:5</span>). It points to the believer’s participation in the new covenant, where cleansing from sin is permanent and based on Christ’s finished work.

Water baptism is closely tied to the **promise of the Holy Spirit**. In <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="2" data-verses="38" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 2:38</span>, Peter calls people to repent, be baptized, and receive the gift of the Holy Spirit. While water baptism publicly signifies the believer’s repentance and faith, the true baptism occurs inwardly when the Holy Spirit washes, regenerates, and seals the believer (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="12" data-verses="13" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 12:13</span>). This spiritual washing makes us members of Christ’s body and empowers us to live holy lives. The **Holy Spirit’s role** in baptism reminds us that salvation is not accomplished through rituals but through God’s transforming power.

It is important to distinguish between the **symbolic act of water baptism** and the spiritual reality of salvation through Christ’s blood. The Bible clearly teaches that salvation is based on faith in Jesus (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="10" data-verses="9-10" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 10:9-10</span>) and not on works, including baptism (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="2" data-verses="8-9" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 2:8-9</span>). However, water baptism is an **act of obedience** that publicly identifies the believer with Christ and the Church. It demonstrates an outward commitment to live as a disciple of Jesus, but it does not add to or complete the work of salvation.

Christ’s water baptism set an example for believers to follow, demonstrating humility, obedience, and preparation for ministry. For Christians today, water baptism serves as a public declaration of faith, symbolizing death to sin and resurrection to new life. While it is a command of Jesus and an important step of obedience, it is not required for salvation. True cleansing occurs through faith in the blood of Jesus, and the empowerment of Christian living comes through the Holy Spirit’s work in the believer. Baptism unites us with Christ and His Church, reminding us of the inward transformation and renewal that God has already accomplished.

Having established water baptism as an outward sign of faith, we now turn to the baptism of the Holy Spirit, which empowers believers for ministry and service.

## Holy Spirit Baptism

The baptism in the Holy Spirit is a profound and transformative experience promised by God to empower believers for ministry, service, and holy living. Every believer receives the Holy Spirit when they’re born again-this is how we’re made spiritually alive. But later, we should also receive the baptism in the Holy Spirit, which empowers us for boldness, spiritual gifts, and effective ministry.

Water baptism is a public declaration of faith and symbolizes the cleansing of sin through Christ’s blood, Spirit baptism represents the empowering presence of God in the believer’s life. Together, these two modes of baptism form complementary aspects of the Christian walk-one as an outward sign of obedience and the other as an inward source of power and renewal. The baptism of the Holy Spirit is the Spirit upon us rather than within. At salvation all believers receive the Spirit within them, but when the Spirit of God comes upon them, they receive power and boldness for ministry.

The baptism in the Holy Spirit was foretold by John the Baptist, who declared, 


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="fragment" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="3" data-verses="11" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Matthew 3:11</u>** *I baptize you with water for repentance, but He who is coming after me… will baptize you with the Holy Spirit and fire”

</span>


This promise pointed to a greater work that Jesus would accomplish after His resurrection-sending the Holy Spirit to dwell within and empower His followers. Jesus confirmed this when He instructed His disciples to wait for the gift of the Spirit, explaining that they would receive power to be His witnesses (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="1" data-verses="8" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 1:8</span>). The fulfillment of this promise occurred on the day of Pentecost, when the disciples were filled with the Holy Spirit, evidenced by speaking in tongues, prophecy, and boldness to proclaim the gospel (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="2" data-verses="1-4" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 2:1-4</span>).

This outpouring marked the beginning of the **Church Age** and demonstrated that the Spirit’s power was now available to all believers. This promise wasn’t limited to the apostles but extends to all who believe, as Peter declared, *“The promise is for you and your children and for all who are far off-for all whom the Lord our God will call”* (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="2" data-verses="39" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 2:39</span>).

### Spirit Within vs Spirit Upon

When someone puts their faith in Jesus, the Holy Spirit gives them new life on the inside. That’s what it means to be born again (**regenerated**). At that moment of faith, the Holy Spirit indwells **WITHIN** the believer, making them spiritually alive and adopting them into the family of God (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="3" data-verses="5-6" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 3:5-6</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Titus" data-chapter="3" data-verses="5" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Titus 3:5</span>). This is the new birth or being born again, as Jesus described in John 3.

Scriptural Basis:

- *<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Titus" data-chapter="3" data-verses="5" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Titus 3:5</span>* - “He saved us through the washing of rebirth and renewal by the Holy Spirit.”

- *<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="1" data-verses="13-14" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 1:13-14</span>* - The Holy Spirit seals believers as a **guarantee** of their inheritance.

- *<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="8" data-verses="9" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 8:9</span>* - “Anyone who does not have the Spirit of Christ does not belong to him.”

This work of regeneration is universal for all believers at salvation and establishes a permanent indwelling of the Spirit. It is not dependent on any outward action, such as water baptism, and guarantees salvation by grace through faith.

In contrast, the baptism in the Holy Spirit is a separate and subsequent experience that equips believers with power for service and witness (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="1" data-verses="8" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 1:8</span>). While regeneration deals with new life and identity, Spirit baptism focuses on empowerment **UPON** and the release of spiritual gifts. This baptism is often accompanied by signs and manifestations of Holy Spirit gifts.

Scriptural Basis:


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="1" data-verses="8" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Acts 1:8</u>** *(NKJV) But you shall receive power when the Holy Spirit has come upon you; and you shall be witnesses to Me in Jerusalem, and in all Judea and Samaria, and to the end of the earth.*

</span>


- *<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="8" data-verses="15-17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 8:15-17</span>* - The Samaritans had believed and were baptized, but they later received the Holy Spirit through prayer and the laying on of hands.

- *<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="19" data-verses="6" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 19:6</span>* - Disciples in Ephesus, already believers, received the Holy Spirit when Paul laid hands on them.

The baptism of the Holy Spirit does not replace regeneration but builds upon it, equipping the believer for spiritual service and boldness in ministry. Being baptized in the Holy Spirit is a free gift to those who are saved, not a requirement to be saved. It is not a requirement that someone manifest a <u>specific</u> gift of the Holy Spirit to prove their ‘baptism’ in the Holy Spirit, but we should expect that the Holy Spirit baptism shows up the same way today as it did in the book of Acts, with signs and boldness accompanying! Some people err in thinking if a person doesn't speak in tongues that they are not saved, or that they do not have the Holy Spirit baptism. Not everyone will receive every gift or manifestation of the Holy Spirit (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="12" data-verses="29-31" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 12:29-31</span>).

## How to Receive the Baptism of the Holy Spirit

The baptism of the Holy Spirit is described in Scripture as a gift that is promised to believers and is received by faith. It is separate from salvation and is primarily for empowerment and witness (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="1" data-verses="8" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 1:8</span>). The baptism of the Holy Spirit is a gift of empowerment for believers, promised by Jesus and fulfilled at Pentecost. It is received through repentance, faith, and asking God. While laying on of hands is often used as a method, it is ultimately God who baptizes with the Spirit.

Scripture presents speaking in tongues and prophecy as a common sign, but the purpose of this baptism is far greater-providing boldness, power, and spiritual gifts for ministry. Every believer is encouraged to seek this experience as part of their spiritual journey, trusting that God will equip them for His work.

The Bible outlines several core principles regarding how this baptism is received:

### **1. The Baptism of the Holy Spirit is a free Gift from God**

The baptism of the Holy Spirit is not something believers can earn-it is a free gift given by God. God wants to give good gifts to His children and is eager to give the Holy Spirit to those who ask with faith and a desire for empowerment.


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Luke" data-chapter="11" data-verses="13" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Luke 11:13</u>** (NKJV) *If you then, being evil, know how to give good gifts to your children, how much more will your heavenly Father give the Holy Spirit to those who ask Him!"*

</span>



<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="2" data-verses="38-39" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Acts 2:38-39</u>** (NKJV) **38** *Then Peter said to them, "Repent, and let every one of you be baptized in the name of Jesus Christ for the remission of sins; and you shall receive the gift of the Holy Spirit. **39** For the promise is to you and to your children, and to all who are afar off, as many as the Lord our God will call."*

</span>


- **2. Ask, Seek, and Receive by Faith**

Scripture emphasizes that the baptism in the Holy Spirit is received through asking in prayer and faith-filled expectation. Faith, combined with a willingness to ask and receive, is the key to experiencing Spirit baptism. If you are not sure if God wants to give you the baptism of the Holy Spirit, ask Him about it, He is a good father! As He prompts you, you may feel a tangible presence of God in your body that you've never experienced before. It’s okay if you don’t! God is a big God, and He doesn't need to be boxed into one perfect formula. If you feel God tugging at your heart, yield to him!

- **<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="8" data-verses="15-17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 8:15-17</span>** - Peter and John prayed and **laid hands** on believers, and they **received the Holy Spirit**.

- **<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="10" data-verses="44-46" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 10:44-46</span>** - Cornelius and his household **received the Holy Spirit** while Peter was preaching-without hands being laid on them-showing that it is ultimately **God who baptizes**.


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="fragment" data-book="Galatians" data-chapter="3" data-verses="2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Galatians 3:2</u>** (NKJV) *… Did you receive the Spirit by the works of the law, or by the hearing of faith?*

</span>


### **3. Laying on of Hands (Optional)**

While some received the Holy Spirit spontaneously (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="10" data-verses="44" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 10:44</span>), others received through the **laying on of hands** by other believers. Laying on of hands is a biblical pattern, but not a requirement. The Holy Spirit ultimately responds to faith, not rituals.


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="8" data-verses="17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Acts 8:17</u>** - *“Then Peter and John placed their hands on them, and they received the Holy Spirit.”*

</span>



<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="19" data-verses="6" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Acts 19:6</u>** - *“When Paul placed his hands on them, the Holy Spirit came on them, and they spoke in tongues and prophesied.”*

</span>


## The Gifts of the Holy Spirit

When the Holy Spirit was poured out, and people baptized in the Holy Spirit, oftentimes manifestations of the Holy Spirit accompanied it.

There are several lists of gifts in the New Testament:

- Motivational Gifts given by God: <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="12" data-verses="6-8" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 12:6-8</span>

  - Discussed in the chapter on the Church.

- Five-Fold Ministry Gifts given by Jesus: <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="4" data-verses="11-13" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 4:11-13</span>

  - Discussed in the chapter on the church.

- Manifestation Gifts given by the Holy Spirit: <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="12" data-verses="8-10" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 12:8-10</span>

  - Discussed here.

Each set of these gifts are given for different purposes, and operate differently, but they collectively point to the **diversity** and **unity** of gifts within the **Body of Christ**. Each gift has a unique role in fulfilling God’s purposes.

### Purpose & Operations of the Gifts

The primary purpose of the gifts of the Holy Spirit is to build up the Body of Christ and bring glory to God:

- **Edification** - Strengthening and encouraging believers (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="14" data-verses="3" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 14:3</span>).

- **Unity** - Promoting harmony and cooperation within the church (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="4" data-verses="12-13" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 4:12-13</span>).

- **Evangelism** - Demonstrating God’s power as a witness to unbelievers (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="2" data-verses="43" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 2:43</span>; <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="8" data-verses="6-7" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 8:6-7</span>).

- **Service** - Meeting the needs of others through acts of mercy and generosity (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="12" data-verses="6-8" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 12:6-8</span>).

These gifts are not for personal glory but for the common good (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="12" data-verses="7" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 12:7</span>). They function best when exercised in love and humility, reflecting the character of Christ (1 Corinthians 13).

Paul emphasized that spiritual gifts must be exercised decently and in order (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="14" data-verses="40" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 14:40</span>). This ensures that the use of gifts promotes unity, peace, and understanding, rather than confusion or division.

- Prophecies should be evaluated (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="14" data-verses="29" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 14:29</span>).

- When giving a message in tongues to the body, pray that it be interpreted (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="14" data-verses="27-28" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 14:27-28</span>).

- Gifts must always bring **edification** to the church (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="14" data-verses="12" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 14:12</span>).

This order does not quench the Spirit but ensures that the gifts operate in ways that **honor God** and **benefit others**.

The operation of spiritual gifts must always be grounded in love. Paul dedicates an entire chapter (1 Corinthians 13) to explaining that without love, even the greatest spiritual gifts are meaningless. Love provides the motivation and framework for using gifts in ways that build up others rather than tearing them down.

Additionally, the gifts operate **as the Spirit wills** (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="12" data-verses="11" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 12:11</span>). They cannot be manufactured or controlled by human effort-they are sovereignly given and empowered by the Holy Spirit, often in moments of need or ministry. <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="13" data-verses="8" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 13:8</span> says that eventually prophecy and tongues will cease. Theologically people who believe that the gifts of the Holy Spirit have ceased are known as ‘Cessationists’. <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="13" data-verses="10" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 13:10</span> says “when the prefect \[Jesus\] comes, that wish is in part will be cause to cease”. As Jesus has not yet returned for His church, the gifts are still in operation on the earth.

It is crucial to recognize that the **gifts of the Spirit** should not be separated from the **fruit of the Spirit** (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Galatians" data-chapter="5" data-verses="22-23" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Galatians 5:22-23</span>). While gifts demonstrate power, the fruit reveals character-love, joy, peace, patience, kindness, goodness, faithfulness, gentleness, and self-control.

- **Gifts without Fruit**: A believer can operate in gifts yet lack maturity or character, leading to pride, misuse, or harm.

- **Fruit without Gifts**: A believer may have godly character but lack the empowerment to minister effectively.

Both are necessary. Gifts equip believers for service. The fruit is a sign of their spiritual health (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="7" data-verses="16" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 7:16</span>) and provide a ‘pleasant taste’ to those who consume it.

### Explaining the 9 Holy Spirit Manifestation Gifts

<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="12" data-verses="8-10" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 12:8-10</span> lists the 9 Holy Spirit manifestation gifts:

1.  **Word of Wisdom** - Supernatural insight into God’s purposes or direction for a situation. It applies divine wisdom to complex problems or decisions (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="6" data-verses="10" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 6:10</span>).

2.  **Word of Knowledge** - A supernatural revelation of information about a person, event, or circumstance that could not have been known naturally (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="4" data-verses="17-18" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 4:17-18</span>).

3.  **Faith** - A supernatural ability to trust God for the miraculous, even in impossible situations. This gift inspires boldness and confidence (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="3" data-verses="6-7" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 3:6-7</span>).

4.  **Gifts of Healing** - The supernatural power to heal physical, emotional, or spiritual illnesses and restore wholeness (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="3" data-verses="6-8" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 3:6-8</span>, <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="9" data-verses="17-18" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 9:17-18</span>).

5.  **Working of Miracles** - The ability to perform supernatural acts that defy natural laws, such as multiplying food or raising the dead (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="9" data-verses="40-41" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 9:40-41</span>, <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="2" data-verses="9-11" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 2:9-11</span>).

6.  **Prophecy** - Declaring God’s truth or will under the inspiration of the Holy Spirit. It is to be encouraging, edifying, and comforting (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="14" data-verses="3" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 14:3</span>).

7.  **Distinguishing (Discernment) Between Spirits** - The ability to discern whether a spiritual influence is from God, the enemy, or human origin (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="16" data-verses="16-18" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 16:16-18</span>).

8.  **Various Kinds of Tongues** - Speaking in languages unknown to the speaker, often as an act of worship, prayer, or a sign to unbelievers (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="2" data-verses="4" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 2:4</span>, <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="14" data-verses="22" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 14:22</span>).

9.  **Interpretation of Tongues** - The supernatural ability to interpret the message spoken in tongues so that the church may be edified (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="14" data-verses="27" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 14:27</span>).

Paul makes it clear that all gifts are valuable and should work together for the common good of the church (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="12" data-verses="7" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 12:7</span>). However, He also encourages believers to desire the greater gifts (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="12" data-verses="31" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 12:31</span>). The greater gifts are often interpreted as those that most directly edify the church, such as prophecy (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="14" data-verses="1-5" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 14:1-5</span>).

- Prophecy is especially emphasized because it strengthens, encourages, and comforts others (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="14" data-verses="3" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 14:3</span>). Paul instructs believers to earnestly desire this gift as it promotes clarity and understanding in the church.

- Gifts like tongues are highly valued for personal edification and prayer (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="14" data-verses="4" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 14:4</span>), but Paul teaches that when giving a message in tongues to the Church to pray for interpretation to ensure they build up the church (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="1 Corinthians" data-chapter="14" data-verses="27-28" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">1 Corinthians 14:27-28</span>).

While the Holy Spirit gives the gifts, we must yield and to submit to the Holy Spirit to use them. Like muscles and talents, when we practice yielding to the Holy Spirit to use His gifts we become more confident and reliable in the use of them.

## Personal Reflection Questions 

1.  How has your understanding of baptism changed after reading this chapter? What significance does it hold in your spiritual journey?

2.  The Old Covenant washings pointed to the need for ultimate cleansing. How does understanding this deepen your appreciation of Christ’s work?

3.  Reflect on your own experience of water baptism or your anticipation of it. How does this act symbolize your identification with Christ’s death, burial, and resurrection?

4.  What does the baptism in the Holy Spirit mean to you personally? How have you experienced or sought His empowerment in your life?

5.  How can the empowerment of the Holy Spirit help you live out your calling and serve others more effectively?

## Group Discussion Questions (20-30 Minutes)

1.  **Icebreaker Question:** “What’s the most memorable baptism service you’ve ever witnessed or experienced? Why was it meaningful?”

2.  **Exploring Old and New Testament Washings:** What do the ceremonial washings of the Old Covenant teach us about holiness and our need for cleansing? How does Jesus fulfill these practices?

3.  **Water Baptism:** Read <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="6" data-verses="3-4" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 6:3-4</span>. How does water baptism symbolize the believer’s union with Christ in His death and resurrection?

4.  **Holy Spirit Baptism:** Read <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="1" data-verses="8" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 1:8</span> and 2:4. What is the purpose of the baptism in the Holy Spirit, and how does it empower believers for ministry?

5.  **Practical Application:** How can we, as a group or individuals, encourage and support others in taking steps of faith like baptism or seeking the Spirit’s empowerment?

# Chapter 12 - Discipleship

Discipleship means following Jesus, learning from Him, and becoming more like Him in how we live and love. It involves a lifelong journey of obedience, transformation, and service, guided by the Holy Spirit, and rooted in God’s Word.

Discipleship is the primary command in the Great Commission. Jesus defined this mission in:


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="28" data-verses="18-20" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Matthew 28:18-20</u>** (NKJV) ***18** And Jesus came and spoke to them, saying, "All authority has been given to Me in heaven and on earth.* ***19** Go therefore and make disciples of all the nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit,* ***20** teaching them to observe all things that I have commanded you; and lo, I am with you always, even to the end of the age." Amen.*

</span>


We, His followers, are to *“go and make disciples of all nations.”* Discipleship is about personal growth and about multiplying that growth by teaching others to follow Christ. Discipleship is the vehicle through which we build the Kingdom of God; the teachings of Jesus are the message.

The call to be a disciple is more than attending church or learning Bible verses-it is an invitation to live like Jesus and to partner with Him in advancing God’s Kingdom. Genuine discipleship helps believers become spiritually mature and effective for ministry (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="4" data-verses="13" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 4:13</span>). It shapes both the heart and skills needed to serve others and share the Gospel. As Paul instructed Timothy in <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="2 Timothy" data-chapter="2" data-verses="2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">2 Timothy 2:2</span>, discipleship follows a pattern of spiritual multiplication, where trained disciples continue raising up new followers to lead other to Christ.

Discipleship is both an individual calling and a global mission. On a personal level, it involves growing in faith, developing an active spiritual life, being in relationship with others & God. At the same time, it fuels the mission of the Church to reach every nation with the Gospel, ensuring that the message of Christ continues to expand and transform lives across generations.

## Why Discipleship Matters

The impact of discipleship goes beyond personal transformation-it has a Kingdom impact. Paul reminded Timothy in <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="2 Timothy" data-chapter="2" data-verses="2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">2 Timothy 2:2</span> to entrust what He had learned to faithful men who would then teach others also. This generational pattern highlights how discipleship is the engine that drives the growth and sustainability of the Church. Discipleship matters because it was Jesus’ chosen method for spreading the Gospel and building His Church.

Rather than focusing on large crowds, Jesus intentionally invested in a small group of followers, teaching them, modeling faithfulness, and preparing them to carry forward His mission. This personal, relational approach ensured that His message would multiply, as each disciple was equipped to make more disciples.

At its core, discipleship is central to fulfilling the Great Commission (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="28" data-verses="19-20" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 28:19-20</span>). Jesus did not just call us to make converts but to make disciples-followers who are grounded in God’s Word, empowered by the Holy Spirit, and equipped for ministry. Through discipleship, believers grow in their knowledge of Scripture, develop spiritual disciplines, and learn to walk in obedience to Christ. It prepares the Church to impact the world, advance the Gospel, and reflect the glory of God to all nations.

### Nature vs Nurture

In the study of biology, psychology, and anthropology we often consider the impact of nature versus nurture. The question often posed, is a person more the result of their innate, in-built genetic heritage? Or are they more the result of their environment and upbringing? The truth is that every person is a blend of nature and nurture. Both elements in strong interplay choreograph the development of a human soul.

When you accepted Jesus, the miracle of salvation made you born-again of incorruptible seed. You received a godly nature! Your spiritual DNA was re-written! This godly nature causes a stirring in our hearts to be close to our heavenly Father, to know Him, to experience Him.

Just as a new-born, whose wonderful, beautiful, innocent nature must be shaped by loving parents to develop into a healthy and mature individual, we too must be shaped and nurtured to be developed.

Discipleship is the process of spiritual development that builds upon our godly nature and makes us spiritually mature. Through investing in discipleship, we ensure that faith is passed down, leaders are raised up, and the Church remains healthy and vibrant-both locally and globally.

### Didactic Model vs Apprentice Model

In modern day western education, the primary relationship the student has is with the material. The student’s learning is focused on content, material, books, videos, assignments, etc. The transformation of education happens via the curriculum. This method of teaching is known as the *didactic model*.

The paradigm of the didactic model has not always been the norm. The model and method used in Jesus’ age was the *apprentice model.* This model of learning emphasizes knowledge being more ‘caught than taught’ and focuses more on the relationship between student and teacher, than student and curriculum.

The didactic model gradually became the default method over about a millennium, from the first medieval universities of Europe in the 11<sup>th</sup> - 13<sup>th</sup> centuries, the invention of the printing press in the 15<sup>th</sup> century, the enlightenment of the 17<sup>th</sup> & 18<sup>th</sup> century, and the impact of industrial revolution on information and education in the 19<sup>th</sup> & 20<sup>th</sup> centuries.

The advantage of the didactic model is that it can scale more efficiently than the apprentice model. Massively open online courses (MOOCs) can have thousands of students at the same time, whereas even a very skilled master can only have perhaps a few dozen apprentices concurrently.

The result of the didactic model is that the student has mastery of content, however the student does not become like the teacher. The teacher is a facilitator for the curriculum. The teacher imparts knowledge.

In the apprentice model the master shares more than knowledge, he imparts wisdom. The result of the apprentice model is that the student has become like the master so completely that the student can reproduce the work of the master and take on apprentices of his own.

The didactic model has its use and place in the Kingdom of God, a good example is this book or any other Christian educational material. Jesus as a rabbi, skilled and trained in the rabbinical method, practiced the apprentice model as the primary model for His disciples.

Jesus describes the apprentice model in:


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Luke" data-chapter="6" data-verses="40" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Luke 6:40</u>** (NKJV) *A <u>disciple</u> is not above his <u>teacher</u>, but everyone who is <u>perfectly trained</u> will be like his teacher.* (Emphasis added)

</span>


In the didactic model the student becomes a carrier of the knowledge, in the apprentice model, the student becomes like the teacher. Let’s study the key words from that passage of Luke.

<table style="width:98%;">
<colgroup>
<col style="width: 31%" />
<col style="width: 67%" />
</colgroup>
<thead>
<tr>
<th colspan="2"><h2 id="table-6---word-study-disciple-teacher-perfectly-trained">Table 6 - Word Study: Disciple, Teacher, Perfectly Trained</h2></th>
</tr>
</thead>
<tbody>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>English</strong></p>
<p><strong>Greek</strong></p>
<p>Pronunciation</p>
<p><strong>Strong’s</strong></p></td>
<td><strong>Definition</strong></td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Disciple</strong></p>
<p><em><strong>Mathētēs</strong></em></p>
<p><em>math-ay-tes'</em></p>
<p><strong>G3101</strong></p></td>
<td>A learner, student, or follower who adheres to and imitates the teacher.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Teacher</strong></p>
<p><em><strong>Didaskalos</strong></em></p>
<p><em>did-as'-kal-os</em></p>
<p><strong>G1320</strong></p></td>
<td>A master, instructor, or one who imparts knowledge and teaches others.</td>
</tr>
<tr>
<td><p><strong>Perfectly Trained</strong></p>
<p><em><strong>Katartizō</strong></em></p>
<p><em>Kat-ar-tid'-zo</em></p>
<p><strong>G2675</strong></p></td>
<td>To equip, prepare, or restore completely; to make mature and fit for purpose.</td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>

To summarize, the purpose and goal of the apprentice model used in discipleship is twofold:

**First, the disciple becomes fully trained**, as the teacher. This does not mean that the disciple imitates everything their teacher does. We aren't being commanded to create clones, but rather to build into maturity men and women who can carry the message of Jesus to the ends of the earth.

**Second, the disciple now becomes a teacher for others**. The disciple must pass along what they've learned to someone else, otherwise the message of the kingdom stops with them. This doesn't mean that they will necessarily have the exact same giftings as their teacher, or that they will enter full time ministry, but it does mean that they must reproduce for their own training to have been complete.

Any organism that does not reproduce is barren, and its line is cut off. An animal that cannot reproduce is not healthy, a church that cannot reproduce is likewise unhealthy.

## Jesus’ Model and Process of Discipleship

Jesus demonstrated the mature and complete example of discipleship in the Gospels with His chosen disciples. We should use these steps broadly as the foundation for our own walk in becoming and making disciples.

The apprentice model has two roles: master and student. Every master has been a student, and hopefully every student will become a master. I will use the terms *disciple* to refer to the student and *disciple-maker* to refer to the master/teacher.

Every discipleship relationship goes through a progression of eight phases. The phases, or steps, are not purely isolated from each other, many times the relationship will flow between the phases. In each phase there is an emphasis for the disciple, and for the disciple-maker. We will study attributes of disciple-makers in depth later in this chapter.

The eight phases of discipleship are:

1.  Invitation/Calling

2.  Teaching

3.  Demonstrating

4.  Participation/Trying

5.  Correction

6.  Empowerment/Authorization

7.  Multiplication

8.  Remain Connected

### Invitation/Calling

Discipleship begins with a personal invitation from Jesus to follow Him. In <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="4" data-verses="19" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 4:19</span>, Jesus called His first disciples, saying, *“Follow me, and I will make you fishers of men.”* This invitation was both relational and transformational-it required leaving behind old ways of life and stepping into a new purpose centered on God’s mission. Jesus’ call wasn’t to spectatorship but to participation in His ministry and Kingdom work.

Modern disciple-makers must invite others into authentic relationships that inspire spiritual growth and obedience. The invitation to discipleship is deeply personal. Jesus meets individuals where they are, addressing their unique needs and circumstances, but He also calls them to go deeper-to grow, serve, and become more like Him. This pattern reflects the relational nature of discipleship, where the focus is not merely on knowledge but on a life-changing relationship with Christ.

### Teaching

Teaching is at the heart of discipleship. Jesus spent much of His ministry teaching the principles of the Kingdom of God, most notably in the Sermon on the Mount (Matthew 5-7). His teachings provided a foundation for living righteously, loving others, and walking in obedience to God. Instruction in the Word of God remains central to discipleship today, as Scripture is the primary tool for spiritual growth, correction, and training in righteousness (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="2 Timothy" data-chapter="3" data-verses="16-17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">2 Timothy 3:16-17</span>).

Disciple-makers must commit to teaching the Bible faithfully, helping others understand and apply it to their lives. Teaching involves more than imparting knowledge-it requires modeling Christ-like living so disciples can see faith in action. Jesus embodied the truths He taught, showing that effective teaching is both verbal and visible. In the same way, disciple-makers should strive to live out what they teach, providing practical examples of obedience and faithfulness.

### Demonstrating

Jesus didn’t just teach His disciples- He demonstrated how to live out the truths He proclaimed. Jesus showed what it means to love, serve, and minister in practical ways. Some examples from the gospels are Jesus washing the disciples’ feet as an act of humility and service (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="13" data-verses="12-15" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 13:12-15</span>), healing the sick, and feeding the hungry. Through His actions, He modeled compassion, faith, and boldness, leaving a clear example for His followers to imitate.

Discipleship requires showing, not just telling. Modern disciple-makers must be transparent about their faith journey, including struggles and victories, so others can see how faith works in real life. Whether it’s praying with someone in need, sharing testimonies, or serving the community, discipleship thrives when it’s rooted in practical demonstration. Actions often speak louder than words, making demonstration a powerful tool for training others in the faith.

### Participation/Trying

Jesus didn’t keep His disciples on the sidelines- He actively involved them in ministry experiences. In <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Luke" data-chapter="9" data-verses="1-6" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Luke 9:1-6</span> and <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="10" data-verses="5-15" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 10:5-15</span>, Jesus sent the disciples out to preach the Gospel, heal the sick, and cast out demons. These hands-on opportunities allowed the disciples to learn by doing, gaining confidence and skills under His guidance.

Modern discipleship should also include practical involvement. Disciple-makers must invite disciples to participate in ministry, whether through leading prayer groups, sharing testimonies, or serving in outreach events. Providing safe spaces for practice allows disciples to grow in boldness while being supported and encouraged. Learning happens best when it is applied, and participation is the key to turning theory into action.

### Correction

Growth often requires correction, and Jesus lovingly corrected His disciples when they misunderstood His teachings or failed in their tasks. In <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Mark" data-chapter="9" data-verses="33-37" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Mark 9:33-37</span>, He addressed their pride by teaching them about servant leadership. In <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Luke" data-chapter="22" data-verses="31-32" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Luke 22:31-32</span>, He prayed for Peter, preparing Him to recover from failure and strengthen others.

Correction in discipleship should be grace-filled and constructive-rooted in Scripture and aimed at restoration (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="2 Timothy" data-chapter="4" data-verses="2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">2 Timothy 4:2</span>). It is not about condemnation but about helping disciples grow and avoid pitfalls. Disciple-makers should provide accountability, using God’s Word to guide and encourage growth while modeling humility in receiving correction themselves.

### Empowerment/Authorization

Jesus didn’t just teach and correct- He empowered His disciples to carry out His mission. He gave them authority to preach the Gospel, heal the sick, and cast out demons (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="28" data-verses="18-20" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 28:18-20</span>). After His resurrection, He promised the Holy Spirit as the source of power for their ministry (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="1" data-verses="8" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 1:8</span>).

Modern discipleship should include empowering others to operate in their spiritual gifts and minister effectively. This involves identifying gifts (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Ephesians" data-chapter="4" data-verses="11-13" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Ephesians 4:11-13</span>), encouraging their use, and releasing disciples to serve. Empowerment also emphasizes reliance on the Holy Spirit for strength, boldness, and wisdom, ensuring disciples are spiritually equipped for the work God has called them to do.

### Multiplication

The goal of discipleship isn’t just personal growth-it’s multiplication. Jesus modeled this by training the disciples to make more disciples (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="28" data-verses="19-20" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 28:19-20</span>). After His resurrection, the apostles continued this multiplying mission, spreading the Gospel and planting churches across the known world. Paul’s instructions in <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="2 Timothy" data-chapter="2" data-verses="2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">2 Timothy 2:2</span> reinforce this principle, urging Timothy to teach others who will teach others.

Discipleship involves equipping disciples to disciple others, creating a chain reaction of faith that expands the Kingdom of God. This process requires intentionality-teaching disciples how to mentor, teach, and train others, while sending them out with confidence and purpose (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Romans" data-chapter="10" data-verses="15" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Romans 10:15</span>).

### Remain Connected

Discipleship doesn’t end when someone is sent out. Jesus modeled ongoing connection with His disciples, continuing to encourage and instruct them even after His resurrection (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="21" data-verses="15-19" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 21:15-19</span>). The early church maintained this pattern, gathering regularly for teaching, fellowship, and prayer (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="2" data-verses="42-47" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 2:42-47</span>).

Modern discipleship should also foster ongoing relationships, providing accountability, encouragement, and support as disciples continue to grow and lead. Maintaining unity in the Body of Christ ensures that disciples are never isolated but remain strengthened and encouraged in their faith journey.

## Who does Discipleship?

Everyone in the body of Christ is responsible first to become a disciple, then to contribute to the process of discipleship making. There are diversities of gifts and functions, so our contributions may look different, but we are all responsible. The people ultimately most responsible for the state and health of discipleship in the local church are the leaders (**elders and deacons - <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="14" data-verses="23" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 14:23</span>**), but everyone plays a part.

Discipleship is a living, growing process-like nurturing a child to maturity. Healthy discipleship brings strength and life to people. Like a parent who strengthens and nourishes a child, and in doing so causes them to be healthy and mature, disciple-makers to help nourish, strengthen, and mature disciples. The end goal of discipleship is not ‘knowledge transfer’, but transformation.

All of us must become perfect (mature) and make disciples. While we will have diverse gifts there are common characteristics for all disciple-makers.

### Characteristics of a Disciple-Maker

A disciple-maker is someone who not only follows Christ but is also committed to helping others grow in their faith. This role requires specific characteristics that reflect both spiritual maturity and practical dedication. Disciple-makers must continually rely on God’s strength, wisdom, and grace to overcome the challenges that come with leading others in their spiritual journeys.

#### Rooted in Christ

The foundation of a disciple-maker’s life must be deeply rooted in Christ. Jesus said, *“I am the vine; you are the branches. If you remain in me and I in you, you will bear much fruit; apart from me you can do nothing”* (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="John" data-chapter="15" data-verses="5" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">John 15:5</span>). Effective disciple-makers know that the ability to grow and help others comes from staying close to Jesus every day. However, resistance to personal growth and spiritual change can hinder this dependence. Disciple-makers must continually cultivate their personal relationship with Jesus through prayer, study of Scripture, and obedience, modeling what it means to abide in Christ for those they are discipling.

#### Committed to the Mission

A disciple-maker must remain committed to the mission of making disciples as commanded by Jesus in the Great Commission (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="28" data-verses="19-20" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 28:19-20</span>). This mission requires intentional focus and prioritization, recognizing that disciple-making is not an optional task but the core purpose of the church. Yet, challenges such as time constraints, busyness, and personal distractions can make it difficult to stay focused. Disciple-makers must intentionally schedule time for mentoring, teaching, and relationship-building, viewing disciple-making as a lifestyle rather than an occasional activity (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Luke" data-chapter="19" data-verses="10" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Luke 19:10</span>).

#### Spirit-Led

Disciple-making cannot be accomplished in human strength alone; it requires the guidance and empowerment of the Holy Spirit. Jesus promised His disciples that they would receive power when the Holy Spirit came upon them (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="1" data-verses="8" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 1:8</span>), equipping them to be witnesses and ministers of the Gospel. Disciple-makers must be sensitive to the Spirit’s leading, relying on His wisdom to navigate complex situations, cultural barriers, and difficult conversations. This reliance on the Holy Spirit enables disciple-makers to operate in love, boldness, and spiritual discernment, ensuring their efforts are Spirit-directed rather than self-directed.

#### Humble and Teachable

Humility is a hallmark of an effective disciple-maker. As Paul instructed, *“Do nothing out of selfish ambition or vain conceit. Rather, in humility value others above yourselves”* (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Philippians" data-chapter="2" data-verses="3" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Philippians 2:3</span>). Disciple-makers recognize that they, too, are lifelong learners who must remain teachable and accountable. They invite correction and feedback, modeling humility for those they mentor. Pride can be a significant barrier to growth, both for disciple-makers and their disciples. Embracing accountability and maintaining a servant’s heart fosters trust and creates an environment for growth and transparency.

#### Patient and Persistent

Discipleship is rarely instantaneous-it is a long-term process that requires patience and persistence. Paul reminds believers to *“not grow weary in doing good, for at the proper time we will reap a harvest if we do not give up”* (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Galatians" data-chapter="6" data-verses="9" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Galatians 6:9</span>). Growth in faith often involves setbacks, questions, and failures, but disciple-makers are called to walk alongside others, offering encouragement and support through every step. It is easy to become discouraged when progress seems slow, but disciple-makers trust that God is at work, even when results are not immediately visible.

Being a disciple-maker requires a blend of spiritual dependence, commitment, and humility. It is a calling that demands patience and persistence while remaining Spirit-led and teachable. Disciple-makers are not expected to be perfect, but they must be faithful-abiding in Christ, investing in others, and trusting God to bring the growth and transformation in His timing. These qualities reflect Christ’s love and help others become mature disciples who, in turn, can disciple others.

## Practical Tools for Discipleship

Effective discipleship requires intentional tools and practices that foster spiritual growth, accountability, and community. These tools help disciples develop a strong foundation in faith, grow in intimacy with God, and become equipped to disciple others. Practical methods such as Bible study, prayer, small groups, and mentorship provide both the structure and support needed for ongoing growth.

### Biblical Study

Engaging deeply with Scripture is the foundation of discipleship. Jesus emphasized the importance of God’s Word in spiritual growth, declaring that *“man shall not live by bread alone, but by every word that proceeds out of the mouth of God”* (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="4" data-verses="4" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 4:4</span>). Regular Bible study and memorization enable disciples to internalize truth, combat falsehoods, and apply God’s wisdom to daily life.

Disciples must be encouraged to immerse themselves in Scripture, not merely as an academic exercise but to know God and transform their hearts (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Psalm" data-chapter="119" data-verses="105" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Psalm 119:105</span>). Practical approaches, such as reading plans, study guides, and group discussions, provide opportunities to explore doctrine and discuss application in community. By building habits of regular study, disciples lay a solid foundation for spiritual maturity and fruitful ministry (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="2 Timothy" data-chapter="3" data-verses="16-17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">2 Timothy 3:16-17</span>).

### Prayer

Prayer is the lifeline of discipleship, connecting believers to God’s presence, power, and guidance. Jesus taught His disciples how to pray in <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Luke" data-chapter="11" data-verses="1-4" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Luke 11:1-4</span>, emphasizing both dependence on God and spiritual alignment with His will. Through prayer, disciples learn to seek God’s wisdom, intercede for others, and receive strength for ministry.

Teaching disciples how to pray-both individually and corporately-equips them to cultivate intimacy with God and discern His leading (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Philippians" data-chapter="4" data-verses="6-7" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Philippians 4:6-7</span>). Incorporating prayer journals, prayer groups, and fasting practices fosters spiritual discipline and empowerment, ensuring disciples remain Spirit-led and mission-focused.

### Small Groups and Community

Discipleship thrives in community. The early church modeled relational discipleship through fellowship, prayer, and breaking bread together (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Acts" data-chapter="2" data-verses="42-47" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Acts 2:42-47</span>). Small groups and accountability partnerships provide a safe space for disciples to grow together, share struggles, and celebrate victories.

The local church serves as the central hub for discipleship relationships, offering opportunities for group Bible studies, prayer meetings, and fellowship events. These gatherings allow disciples to practice love, bear one another’s burdens (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Galatians" data-chapter="6" data-verses="2" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Galatians 6:2</span>), and encourage one another toward spiritual maturity (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Hebrews" data-chapter="10" data-verses="24-25" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Hebrews 10:24-25</span>). Small groups also provide a framework for multiplying leaders, as mature disciples train and send others to lead groups and mentor new believers.

### Mentorship

Mentorship provides a personal and intentional approach to discipleship. Jesus modeled this by investing deeply in the twelve disciples, walking with them through teaching, correction, and encouragement. Similarly, one-on-one relationships allow disciple-makers to tailor guidance to individual needs, helping disciples develop in faith, character, and leadership.

Mentors serve as accountability partners, offering support, prayer, and wisdom during times of growth and struggle. Practical tools like spiritual assessments, goal-setting, and regular check-ins ensure that disciples stay focused and motivated. Peer relationships within mentorship structures also foster mutual growth, as both mentor and disciple learn from shared experiences (<span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Proverbs" data-chapter="27" data-verses="17" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Proverbs 27:17</span>).

## **Personal Reflection Questions**

1.  What does it mean to you personally to be a disciple of Jesus? How are you actively following Him in your daily life?

2.  Reflect on Jesus’ model of discipleship. Which aspect of His approach-inviting, teaching, demonstrating, or empowering-stands out most to you, and why?

3.  How can you multiply your faith by discipling others? What challenges or fears might hold you back, and how can you overcome them? (Notice I said multiply your faith, not multiply your disciples!)

4.  What characteristics of a disciple-maker (e.g., patience, humility, relational focus) do you see in yourself? Which traits do you feel led to develop further?

5.  Think about the people in your life. Who might God be calling you to disciple? How can you take the first step in building that relationship?

## Group Discussion Questions (20-30 Minutes)

1.  **Icebreaker Question:** Who has had the greatest influence on your spiritual growth, and what did they do that impacted you?

2.  **Exploring Jesus’ Model:** Read <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="4" data-verses="19" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 4:19</span>. How does Jesus’ invitation to ‘follow Me’ encapsulate the heart of discipleship? What does this look like today?

3.  **The Call to Make Disciples:** Discuss <span class="x-scripture" data-type="ref" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="28" data-verses="19-20" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">Matthew 28:19-20</span>. Why is making disciples central to the mission of the church? How can we, as individuals, participate in this mission?

4.  **Overcoming Challenges:** What are some common barriers to disciple-making (e.g., time, fear, lack of confidence)? How can we support one another in overcoming these barriers?

5.  **Practical Application:** What is one specific step you can take this week to invest in someone’s spiritual growth? How can we, as a group, encourage each other in this process?

# Chapter 13 - Spiritual Authority

Jesus is the ultimate and complete source of spiritual authority. As the triumphant Christ He has final authority over all things in heaven and the earth.


<span class="x-scripture" data-type="quote" data-book="Matthew" data-chapter="28" data-verses="18-20" data-lang="en" data-version="NKJV">

> **<u>Matthew 28:18-20</u>** (NKJV) ***18** And Jesus came and spoke to them, saying, "All authority has been given to Me in heaven and on earth.* ***19** Go therefore and make disciples of all the nations, baptizing them in the name of the Father and of the Son and of the Holy Spirit,* ***20** teaching them to observe all things that I have commanded you; and lo, I am with you always, even to the end of the age." Amen.*

</span>


In this chapter we will study the authority and power of Christ, which authorities and powers He has delegated to us (His church), and how to operate in the authority of the name of Jesus. We will also study angelic and demonic beings and examine their authority, power, and natures.

Authority means having the right to act. Power is the ability to carry it out. Both are essential in the spiritual life. Authority is different from power. Let’s look at the following table which compares authority & power and consider a few examples.

</blockquote></td>
